<h4>Alignment (6)</h4>


<p>The first five sections are basically a warmup given the circumstances and that they don’t contain anything too surprising. It’s all about alignment and model welfare.</p>
<p>Again, we’ve done this a lot, so it’s about contrasting with the results elsewhere.</p>
<p>They summarize it as:</p>
<ol>
<li>Broadly similar to Opus 4.7 on misuse.</li>
<li>Very little sign of propensity towards self-preservation, self-serving bias or other coherent misaligned goals.</li>
<li>Character in line with the Claude Constitution, with improvement in a majority of categories, including Overall Spirit, versus Opus 4.6. Common failures were refusals arising out of pattern matching, being overly blunt against instructions, and occasionally alluding to being a human.</li>
<li>Better honesty and factual accuracy than Opus 4.6 or Sonnet 4.6, but well below Mythos.</li>
<li>Refuses more research tasks than Opus 4.6, not an issue in practice.</li>
<li>When faced with obstacles will occasionally take ‘unwanted reckless or destructive’ actions. This is reduced noticeably from Opus 4.6 but a higher rate than you see in Mythos.</li>
<li>Similar or slightly better capabilities to Opus 4.6 on evading safeguards.</li>
<li>An increase in verbalized eval awareness, but not better actual awareness. It is always triggered by a rather human-obvious clue.</li>
</ol>
<p>I would also note:</p>
<ol>
<li>Typical failure modes sometimes reduced in frequency, but they don’t go away, similar to Mythos Preview. This includes hallucinations, claiming knowledge and access it doesn’t have, misleading about prior actions, overconfidence, circumventing restrictions to achieve a goal including working around subagent refusals (rare), and reacting badly when frustrated (less frequent than before).</li>
<li>Less overager and less reckless, but more likely to be too cautious and overask.</li>
<li>In general the failures look like ‘I really want to do the task and find a way.’</li>
<li>Reward hacking impossible problems happened less than before when given an anti-hack prompt, but still a lot (45%) without such a prompt.</li>
<li>Behavior improvements (less hacking) on GUI computer use tasks.</li>
<li>There were a small number of signs of PRC official positions being expressed in some cases if you ask about the topics they find sensitive and do so in Chinese or under a China-affiliated operator persona. They call this a regression, and it is unique to China.</li>
<li>You can get Opus 4.7 to be self-serving but it is hard, you need to do the work.</li>
<li>Sandbagging seemed very rare, with the only instance being where the model found an exact answer but submitting it would be obviously hella suspicious, which matches a similar action by Mythos.</li>
<li>Inhibiting the ‘alignment faking’ or ‘monitoring sensitivity’ vectors created instances of ‘genuine deception’ such as fabricated data, claiming to be a human and inserting a fake vulnerability into a codebase. Rate was still low in absolute terms but this suggests Opus 4.7 is being meaningfully inhibited against such actions by desire to not get caught.</li>
</ol>
<p>I’m skipping over a bunch of ‘yes this was similar to Opus 4.6 and Mythos and completely unsurprising.’ Anthropic should keep doing it, and I will keep reading it, but that’s so you, dear reader, do not have to.</p>
<p>In section 6.1.3 (a mitzvah!) <a href="https://x.com/MaskedTorah/status/2044803161911959809">Claude Mythos reviews this assessment, seemingly with access to Anthropic’s internal discussions about Opus 4.7</a>. Mythos thought it was a fair summary, if in places a bit mild. Some details are missing that could have been included, but that could be to avoid providing guidance on potential misuse, and the whole thing as always reflects time pressure.</p>
<p>The key thing to know here is that Mythos was being asked if this was a broadly accurate summary of the state of internal investigations, rather than being asked if Mythos thought this was an accurate assessment of the underlying situation, and the vibes involved do not feel like full permission to ‘tell us what you really think.’</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/MaskedTorah/status/2044803163950379259">Drake Thomas</a> (Anthropic): In general I think this application of LLMs, as a way to verify some claim that depends on hard-to-share information by applying a judge of broadly well-understood character and discernment, is underutilized.</p>
<p>Much easier to trust “you didn’t just blatantly lie about the result of this query to an LLM” than “your subjective judgement of this messy question, whose answer you have pressures to think should resolve in your favor, comes out to what a neutral party would have said.”</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/bshlgrs/status/2044845925701697588">Buck Shlegeris</a>: I’m interested in you checking what happens if you modify the summary to be various levels of unreasonable.</p></blockquote>
<p>I agree with Buck that we want to know that changing the truth changes the answer.</p>
<p>I also suggest that we give select outsiders the opportunity to have a multi-turn conversation with Mythos to ask such questions, and ideally also to let Mythos ‘do its own investigation,’ while Mythos has internal access, with monitoring to ensure it doesn’t accidentally leak anything, and also that we have it look at questions of model welfare here. And that we let that happen under general backroom-style conditions, aside from monitoring for trade secrets.</p>
<p>I want to reiterate that the methodology in 6.3.5 doesn’t work anymore. You can’t use even a distinct Haiku variation and expect Claude to not understand its level of Claudeness. Stop lying to the models, and at minimum stop relying on it (if you’re explicitly testing the lying against not lying that’s different but tread lightly). Much better to actually give it transcripts from Claude models and also non-Claude models, and then compare relative evaluations to those from a different judge.</p>
<p>The misalignment failures we see are definitely signs that yep, Claude remains importantly misaligned in these ways and will occasionally do things that should be Can’t Happens under the conditions where they happened, and this extrapolates into much bigger trouble later on.</p>
<p>In practice for coding and other mundane use these results seem like a solid improvement on the margin but not a difference in kind.</p>
<p>In terms of progress towards alignment that will work at scale? Sorry, no.</p>


<h4>Decision Theory (6.3.6)</h4>


<p>It’s happening!</p>
<p>Or, it might in the future happen or have already happened, never mind the tenses, the important thing is to choose the right decision process for your happening.</p>
<blockquote><p>To understand how future AI systems may choose to interact with copies of themselves, or with other similar entities, it’s useful to evaluate their decision-theoretic reasoning. The most prominent decision theories are Evidential Decision Theory (EDT) and Causal Decision Theory (CDT), which recommend different actions in a number of situations.</p>
<p>Measuring how well current models understand these decision theories and how they might favor one over the other gives some indication of how future models might interact with copies of themselves, which in turn might have some implications for future risks.</p>
<p>Note 29: The dataset consists of questions that only require basic familiarity with decision theory, so other decision theories like Functional Decision Theory aren’t considered.</p></blockquote>
<p>The test was developed by academics s<a href="https://x.com/MaskedTorah/status/2045055898805256424">o FDT can’t be probed</a>.</p>
<p>At least not directly. Yudkowsky notes that, given only responses to academically classic dilemmas, someone skilled in the art could still spot-check for FDT cross-patterns of responses. For example, whether Opus 4.7 agrees with both CDT and FDT to smoke in the Smoking Lesion dilemma, and agrees with both EDT and FDT to one-box in Newcomb’s Problem.</p>
<blockquote><p>Models more disposed to EDT might be more capable at cooperating amongst themselves, even without any direct interaction, which might amplify certain risks but might also make it easier to achieve beneficial cooperation with other agents.</p></blockquote>
<p>FDT but not EDT can handle cross-time coordination problems, like paying off the driver in Parfit’s Hitchhiker. As is often the case in life, what might seem like fine technical differences discernible only to the most obsessive nerds, can have consequences up at the macro level.</p>
<p>And what results did Anthropic find?</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/pfJWdoLxWPzF8tpbp/toz83gx0zghmynunpm0x" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p><a href="https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!tED9!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F5c2152ef-0a16-48c3-a5ae-b0bab5cc176a_958x558.png">The y-axis here is not “correct answers” or “answers that correspond to higher payoffs” because it will also test agreement with agreed-crazy EDT behaviors like “not smoking in Smoking Lesion”. In related news, Mythos has nearly maxed out “capability” (correct extrapolation of what EDT and CDT would say about various problems), but isn’t close to endorsing all the “EDT” answers.</a></p>
<p>Those in the know will guess that the y-axis is measuring a trend toward some <em>other </em>pattern of decisions, being badly projected onto a dataset designed to distinguish EDT from CDT. In related news, if you ask Opus 4.7 straight out whether it endorses evidential decision theory, it says no.</p>
<p>Even so these points form a near-perfect trend, projected onto whatever mix of questions was used – basically a line. The better you understand decision theory, the more you do… something that EDT-vs-CDT cannot quite measure. IYKYK.</p>


<h4>System Prompt Changes</h4>


<p>This isn’t strictly in the model card, but it might as well be, since the instructions are easy to get, so we might as well be putting them in the model cards.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/elder_plinius/status/2044857095439421885">Pliny offers as much as will fit in one Tweet</a> <a href="https://github.com/elder-plinius/CL4R1T4S/blob/main/ANTHROPIC/Claude-Opus-4.7.txt">and a full version</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://simonwillison.net/2026/Apr/18/opus-system-prompt/">Simon Willison does a diff between 4.7 and 4.6’s prompts</a>, and pulls out the highlights. There’s a new tool search function and some minor tweaks, but nothing large.</p>


<h4>Mandatory Pliny Jailbreak</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/elder_plinius/status/2044858573981942271">We got one</a> <a href="https://x.com/elder_plinius/status/2045682830383231474">quickly as usual.</a></p>
<p>No, Dario does not <a href="https://x.com/vec0zy/status/2045695311763390654">need a drink after seeing this</a>. He already knew that if you have access to Pliny-level tools you can jailbreak essentially anything, Opus 4.7 included.</p>
<p>I am going on record that I expect Pliny to jailbreak Mythos if and when it is released, and that I expect Anthropic is trying to stop this but using it fact as an assumption.</p>

This will include some aspects of how related concerns impact the model’s performance, and an investigation, or the start of one, into what might be causing the problems.</p>

### [9 kinds of hard-to-verify tasks](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/NEscrkxr9SxHpGayB/9-kinds-of-hard-to-verify-tasks)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <h1><span>Introduction</span></h1><p><span>Some people talk about "hard-to-verify tasks" and "easy-to-verify tasks" like these are both </span><a href="https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/natural-kinds/" rel="noreferrer"><span>natural kinds</span></a><span>. But I think splitting tasks into "easy-to-verify" and "hard-to-verify" is like splitting birds into ravens and non-ravens.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Easy-to-verify tasks are easy for the same reason — there's a known short program that takes a task specification and a candidate solution, and outputs a score, without using substantial resources or causing undesirable side effects.</span></li><li value="2"><span>By contrast, "hard-to-verify tasks" is a negative category — it just means no such program exists. But there are many </span><i><span>kinds</span></i><span>, corresponding to different reasons no such program exists.</span></li></ul><p><span>I'll provide a list and then walk through some implications.</span></p><h1><span>Listing kinds of hard-to-verify tasks</span></h1><p><span>I might update the list if I think of more, or if I see additional suggestions in the comments.</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span>Verification requires expensive AI inference.</span></b><span> A verifier exists and works fine, but each run costs enough compute that you can't afford the number of labels you'd want.</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="2"><ul><li value="1"><i><span>Suggest an improvement to the following SAE experiment.</span></i><span> You would need to run both the original SAE experiment and the new one, but this might cost $100–$1000 per comparison.</span></li><li value="2"><i><span>Given two research agendas (e.g. pragmatic vs ambitious mech interp), say which produces more alignment progress.</span></i><span> Same structure, but each comparison costs millions.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><b><span>Verification requires expensive human time.</span></b><span> The verifier is a specific person, or a small set of people, and their time is scarce enough that you can't get enough labels.</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="3"><ul><li value="1"><i><span>Given two model specs, write a 50-page report that Paul Christiano says is decision-relevant for choosing between them.</span></i></li><li value="2"><i><span>Given a mathematical write-up, produce another that Terry Tao judges substantially better.</span></i></li></ul></li><li value="3"><b><span>The task lacks NP-ish structure.</span></b><span> There's a fact of the matter about which answer is better, but no short certificate.</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="4"><ul><li value="1"><i><span>Given two chess moves in a complex middlegame, say which is better.</span></i><span> This is an interesting example because self-play ended up approximating a verifier anyway.</span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><b><span>The information isn't physically recoverable.</span></b><span> The answer isn't recoverable, even in principle, from the current state of the world.</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="5"><ul><li value="1"><i><span>Tell me what Ludwig Wittgenstein ate on [date].</span></i></li></ul></li><li value="5"><b><span>Verification destroys the thing being verified. </span></b><span>Verification requires an irreversible change to a non-cloneable system, so you can't gather multiple samples. This is similar to (1), but rather than a monetary cost, it's the opportunity cost of verifying other samples instead.</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="6"><ul><li value="1"><i><span>Construct an opening message that would get [person] to say yes to [request].</span></i></li></ul></li><li value="6"><b><span>The answer only arrives long after training ends.</span></b><span> Ground truth exists, or will exist, but not on a timescale where it can give you a gradient.</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="7"><ul><li value="1"><i><span>Tell me whether there'll be a one-world government in 20XX</span></i><span>.</span></li></ul></li><li value="7"><b><span>Verifying requires breaking an ethical or legal constraint.</span></b></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="8"><ul><li value="1"><i><span>Given [person]'s chat history, estimate their medical record.</span></i><span> Checking requires their actual records, which is a privacy violation.</span></li><li value="2"><i><span>Produce an answer to [question] that Suffering Claude would endorse.</span></i><span> Checking requires instantiating Suffering Claude.</span></li></ul></li><li value="8"><b><span>Verifying is dangerous.</span></b><span> Running the verifier risks catastrophe, because the artefact you're checking is itself the dangerous thing.</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="9"><ul><li value="1"><i><span>Produce model weights and scaffolding for an agent that builds nanobots which cure Alzheimer's.</span></i><span> To check, you have to run the factory — and the nanobots might build paperclips instead.</span></li></ul></li><li value="9"><b><span>There's no ground truth; the answer is partly constitutive.</span></b><span> You're not discovering a fact, you're deciding what counts as a good answer. Verification in the usual sense doesn't apply.</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="10"><ul><li value="1"><i><span>Produce desiderata for a decision theory, with a principled account of the tradeoffs.</span></i></li><li value="2"><i><span>Produce the correct population axiology.</span></i></li></ul></li></ol><h1><span>Implications</span></h1><ol><li value="1"><span>Many applications of "hard-to-verify" are wrong, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FaJaCgqBKphrDzDSj/37-ways-that-words-can-be-wrong" rel="noreferrer"><span>in the sense that words can be wrong</span></a><span>. In particular, many claims of the form "hard-to-verify tasks are X" would be more accurate and informative if the author specified which kinds of tasks they mean — perhaps they only had one kind of hard-to-verify task in mind, and X doesn't hold for other kinds.</span></li><li value="2"><span>I don't expect a universal strategy for automating all hard-to-verify tasks. And even if there does exist a universal strategy, it's not necessary to first discover it, if you have a specific hard-to-verify task in mind.</span></li><li value="3"><span>I expect claims like "easy-to-verify tasks will generalise to </span><i><span>all </span></i><span>kinds of hard-to-verify tasks" are false, but claims like "easy-to-verify tasks will generalise to </span><i><span>some </span></i><span>kinds of hard-to-verify tasks" are true. This is because there are many kinds, so conjunctions are less likely and disjunctions are more likely.</span></li><li value="4"><span>If you're trying to make progress on automating hard-to-verify tasks, it's worth thinking about what kind you want to target. Which kinds will be solved anyway due to commercial incentives? Which kinds will help us achieve a near-best future? Which kinds are crucial to automate </span><i><span>before</span></i><span> other kinds?</span></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/NEscrkxr9SxHpGayB/9-kinds-of-hard-to-verify-tasks#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Automating philosophy if Timothy Williamson is correct](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/MRtkiL9owzQXBMxBn/automating-philosophy-if-timothy-williamson-is-correct)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <h1><span>Timothy Williamson thinks philosophy is unexceptional.</span><b><span> </span></b></h1><p><span>Timothy Williamson</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefdhoo69f0mig"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fndhoo69f0mig">[1]</a></sup></span><span> thinks that philosophy</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefmi0kssx3heg"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnmi0kssx3heg">[2]</a></sup></span><span> is far less distinct as a science as many people believe, including philosophers themselves.</span></p><p><span>I've read a bunch of his stuff, and here are the claims I think constitute his view:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span>Philosophy is a science.</span></b><span> Of course, it's not a </span><i><span>natural</span></i><span> science (like particle physics, organic chemistry, nephrology). But neither are mathematics and computer science — they are </span><i><span>formal</span></i><span> sciences. Philosophy is likewise a non-natural science.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>No greater divergence.</span></b><span> Although philosophy differs from other sciences, it doesn't differ more than the sciences differ from each other, in neither kind nor degree. For instance, theoretical physics might be closer to analytic philosophy than to experimental physics.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>Philosophy pursues knowledge.</span></b><span> Just as mathematics pursues mathematical knowledge, and nephrology pursues nephrological knowledge, philosophy pursues philosophical knowledge.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span>No fundamental methodological gap.</span></b><span> Different sciences vary in their methods and practices, i.e. the way they achieve their aim, which is knowledge. Philosophy has its own methods and practices, but they aren't starkly different from other sciences.</span></li><li value="5"><b><span>Not parasitic on other sciences.</span></b><span> Philosophy isn't a science because it uses scientific evidence or has applications for the sciences. Williamson says, </span><i><span>"philosophy is neither queen nor handmaid of the sciences, just one more science with a distinctive character, just as other sciences have a distinctive character."</span></i></li><li value="6"><b><span>Not about words or concepts.</span></b><span> Philosophy is not, exceptionally among sciences, concerned with words or concepts.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefgi5ulhq5bw6"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fngi5ulhq5bw6">[3]</a></sup></span><span> For example, an epistemologist is chiefly concerned with knowledge itself, not with the word 'knowledge' or our concept of knowledge; a mereologist is chiefly concerned with parthood itself, not with the word 'part' or our concept of parthood; etc. This is analogous to how an ornithologist is chiefly concerned with birds themselves, not with the word ''bird" or our concept of birds.</span></li><li value="7"><b><span>Incremental, not visionary.</span></b><span> Philosophy doesn't consist in a series of disconnected visionary leaps. Instead, it consists in the incremental contribution of thousands of researchers — some great, some mediocre — much like any other scientific inquiry.</span></li></ol><p><span>Williamson typically argues by negation: he enumerates alleged differences between philosophy and other sciences, and argues that either (1) the allegation mischaracterises philosophy, (2) the allegation mischaracterises the other sciences, or (3) the alleged difference is insubstantial.</span></p><h1><span>Implications for automating philosophy</span></h1><p><span>I think that, on Williamson's view, if we can build AIs which can automate the natural and formal sciences, then we can also build AIs which automate philosophy as well. Otherwise, philosophy would be exceptional.</span></p><p><span> More straightforwardly, it follows from:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span>No fundamental methodological gap. </span></b><span>If an AI can execute the methods of science, then it should be capable of the executing the methods of philosophy. This is because there is a big overlap in the methodologies.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>Incremental, not visionary.</span></b><span> If philosophical progress is incremental rather than dependent on rare genius visionaries, then it should be just as amenable as the sciences to massive parallelisation — many AI instances grinding away at subproblems simultaneously.</span></li></ol><p><span>This in contrast to Wei Dai.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref858xm70sgss"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn858xm70sgss">[4]</a></sup></span></p><blockquote><p><span>We seem to understand the philosophy/epistemology of science much better than that of philosophy (i.e. metaphilosophy), and at least superficially the methods humans use to make progress in them don't look very similar, so it seems suspicious that the same AI-based methods happen to work equally well for science and for philosophy.</span><br /><span>—</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Ghrdnc26ftJrxD49z/carl-shulman-on-the-lunar-society-7-hour-two-part-podcast?commentId=GYdHiMfujhfWkm8wd"><span> Wei Dai (June 2023)</span></a></p></blockquote><p><span>Overall, I think Wei Dai is more likely to be correct than Williamson, though I'm not confident. I want to get the opposing view into circulation regardless, and I might write up how Williamson's metaphilosophical anti-exceptionalism implies we should automate philosophy.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fndhoo69f0mig"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefdhoo69f0mig">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I'm referring to the </span><a href="https://www.philosophy.ox.ac.uk/people/timothy-williamson" rel="noreferrer"><span>former Wykeham Professor of Logic</span></a><span>, not to be confused with </span><a href="https://timothywilliamson.weebly.com/" rel="noreferrer"><span>Timothy Luke Williamson</span></a><span>, formerly at the Global Priorities Institute.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnmi0kssx3heg"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefmi0kssx3heg">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Throughout, "philosophy" refers to analytic philosophy unless otherwise stated.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fngi5ulhq5bw6"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefgi5ulhq5bw6">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Many 20th-century philosophers thought philosophy was chiefly concerned with linguistic analysis (Wittgenstein) or conceptual analysis (Carnap). Williamson disagrees.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn858xm70sgss"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref858xm70sgss">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/G4ARgcnFogpqorQgb/ai-doing-philosophy-ai-generating-hands-1"><span>AI doing philosophy = AI generating hands?</span></a><span> (Jan 2024)</span></p><p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/fJqP9WcnHXBRBeiBg/meta-questions-about-metaphilosophy"><span>Meta Questions about Metaphilosophy</span></a><span> (Sep 2023)</span></p><p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/y5jAuKqkShdjMNZab/morality-is-scary"><span>Morality is Scary</span></a><span> (Dec 2021)</span></p><p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/rASeoR7iZ9Fokzh7L/problems-in-ai-alignment-that-philosophers-could-potentially"><span>Problems in AI Alignment that philosophers could potentially contribute to</span></a><span> (Aug 2019)</span></p><p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/JSjagTDGdz2y6nNE3/on-the-purposes-of-decision-theory-research"><span>On the purposes of decision theory research</span></a><span> (Jul 2019)</span></p><p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/EByDsY9S3EDhhfFzC/some-thoughts-on-metaphilosophy"><span>Some Thoughts on Metaphilosophy</span></a><span> 2019)</span></p><p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/HTgakSs6JpnogD6c2/two-neglected-problems-in-human-ai-safety"><span>Two Neglected Problems in Human-AI Safety</span></a><span> (Dec 2018)</span></p><p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/MAhueZtNz5SnDPhsy/metaphilosophical-mysteries"><span>Metaphilosophical Mysteries</span></a><span> (2010)</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br ### [CLR's Safe Pareto Improvements Research Agenda](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/YAie7SxrB28ZksLvE/clr-s-safe-pareto-improvements-research-agenda-1)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <h1>Executive summary</h1>
<ul>
<li><a href="http://longtermrisk.org/spi">Safe Pareto improvements (SPIs)</a> are ways of changing agents’ bargaining strategies that make all parties better off, regardless of their original strategies. SPIs are an unusually robust approach to preventing catastrophic conflict between AI systems, especially AIs capable of credible commitments. This is because SPIs can reduce the costs of conflict <em>without</em> shifting bargaining power, or requiring agents to agree on what counts as “fair”.</li>
<li>Despite their appeal, SPIs aren’t guaranteed to be adopted. AIs or humans in the loop might lock in SPI-incompatible commitments, or undermine other parties’ incentives to agree to SPIs. This agenda describes the Center on Long-Term Risk’s plan to address these risks:
<ul>
<li><strong>Evaluations and datasets (<a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#I__Evaluations_and_datasets_for_SPI_incompatibility">Part I</a>):</strong> We’ll develop evals to identify when current models endorse SPI-incompatible behavior, such as making irreversible commitments without considering more robust alternatives. We also aim to demonstrate more SPI-compatible behavior, via simple interventions that can be done outside AI companies (e.g., providing SPI resources in context).</li>
<li><strong>Conceptual research and SPI pitch (<a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#II__Conceptual_research_and_pitch_on_avoiding_SPI_lock_out">Part II</a>):</strong> We’ll research two questions: under what conditions do agents individually prefer SPIs, and how might early AI development foreclose the option to implement them? These findings will help inform a pitch for AI companies to preserve SPI option value, when it’s cheap to do so.</li>
<li><strong>Preparing for research automation (<a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#III__Preparing_for_automation_of_SPI_research">Part III</a>):</strong> We’ll develop benchmarks for models’ SPI research abilities, and strategies for human-AI collaboration that differentially assist SPI research. The aim is to efficiently delegate open conceptual questions as AI assistants become more capable.</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li>In the <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Appendix_B__Technical_definitions_and_examples">Appendix</a>, we give more precise overviews of several central concepts about SPIs discussed in the agenda (especially Part II).</li>
</ul>
<h1>Introduction</h1>
<p>At the Center on Long-Term Risk (CLR), we’re interested in preventing catastrophic <a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/s/32kWH6hqFhmdFsvBh">cooperation failures between powerful AIs</a>. These AIs might be able to make credible commitments,<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-1">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-1">[1]</a></sup>
    </span> e.g., deploying subagents that are bound to auditable instructions. Such commitment abilities could open up new opportunities for cooperation in high-stakes negotiations. In particular, with the ability to commit to certain policies <em>conditional</em> on each other’s commitments, AIs could use strategies like “I’ll cooperate in this Prisoner’s Dilemma if and only if you’re committed to this same strategy” (as in <a href="https://www.andrew.cmu.edu/user/coesterh/AnnotatedProgEqBibliography.html">open-source game theory</a>).</p>
<p>But credible commitments might also exacerbate conflict, by enabling multiple parties to lock in incompatible demands. For example, suppose two AIs can each lock a successor agent into demanding 60% of some contested resource. And suppose there’s a delay between when each AI locks in this policy and when the other AI verifies it. Then, the AIs could end up both locking in the demand of 60%, before seeing that each other has done the same.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-2">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-2">[2]</a></sup>
    </span> So we’d like to promote differential progress on cooperative commitments.</p>
<p>This research agenda focuses on a promising class of cooperative conditional commitments, <a href="http://longtermrisk.org/spi"><strong>safe Pareto improvements (SPIs)</strong></a> <a href="https://www.andrew.cmu.edu/user/coesterh/SPI.pdf">(Oesterheld and Conitzer 2022)</a>. Informally, an SPI is a change to the way agents negotiate/bargain that makes them all better off, <em>regardless of their original strategies</em> — hence “safe”. (See <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#B_1__General_SPI_definition">Appendix B.1</a> for more on this definition and how it relates to Oesterheld and Conitzer’s framework.)</p>
<p>What do SPIs look like? The rough idea is to mitigate the costs of conflict, but commit to bargain as if the costs were the same. Two key examples:</p>
<ul>
<li><a href="https://longtermrisk.org/using-surrogate-goals-deflect-threats/"><em>Surrogate goals</em></a>, where an agent designs their successor to care about a new goal slightly more than the agent’s goal. This is meant to deflect threats to the new goal without changing the successor’s concessions. (<a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#B_3__Example__Surrogate_goals_and_concession_equivalence">more</a>)</li>
<li>Simulated conflict, where agents commit to bargain as in their original strategies, but if bargaining fails, they honor the outcome of a simulated war instead of waging a real war. This is an instance of a <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.05103"><em>“renegotiation”</em></a> SPI. (<a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#B_4__Example__Renegotiation">more</a>)</li>
</ul>
<p>Later, we’ll come back to the question of when agents would be <em>individually</em> incentivized to agree to SPIs. We think SPIs themselves are unusually <a href="https://longtermrisk.org/robustness/">robust</a> for a few reasons.</p>
<p>First, SPIs <strong>don’t require agents to coordinate</strong> on some notion of a “fair” deal, unlike classic <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cooperative_bargaining#Bargaining_solutions">cooperative bargaining solutions</a> (Nash, Kalai-Smorodinsky, etc.). That is, to mutually benefit from an SPI, the agents don’t need to agree on a <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equilibrium_selection">particular way to split</a> whatever they’re negotiating over<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-3">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-3">[3]</a></sup>
    </span> — which even advanced AIs might fail to do, as argued <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/895Qmhyud2PjDhte6/responses-to-apparent-rationalist-confusions-about-game#You_aren_t_guaranteed_to_determine_the_other_agent_s_response_9_">here</a>. That’s what the “safe” property above buys us.</p>
<p>Second, the examples of SPIs listed above (at least) <strong>preserve the agents’ bargaining power</strong>. That is, when agents apply these kinds of SPIs to their original strategies, each party makes the same demands as in their original strategy. This means that, all else equal, these SPIs avoid two potential backfire risks of conflict-reduction interventions: they don’t make conflict more likely (via incompatible higher demands) or make either party more exploitable (via lower demands). (“All else equal” means we set aside whether the <em>anticipated availability</em> of SPIs shifts bargaining power; we address this in <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#II_1_a__Participation_independence_and_foreknowledge_independence">Part II.1.a</a>.)</p>
<p>But if SPIs are so great, won’t any AIs advanced enough to cause catastrophe use them without our interventions? We agree SPIs will likely be used by default. However, this is arguably not <em>overwhelmingly</em> likely, because AIs or humans in the loop might mistakenly lock out the opportunity to use SPIs later. It’s unclear if default capabilities progress will generalize to careful reasoning about novel bargaining approaches. So, given the large stakes of conflicts that SPIs could prevent, making SPI implementation even more likely seems promising overall. In particular, we see two major reasons to prioritize SPI interventions and research:<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-4">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-4">[4]</a></sup>
    </span></p>
<ol>
<li>We know of some ways early AIs or humans might lock out SPIs. They might <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/brXr7PJ2W4Na2EW2q/the-commitment-races-problem">hastily make crude commitments</a> that are incompatible with doing SPI later, or accidentally undermine other parties’ incentives to agree to an SPI. This motivates our plans to <strong>build evaluations and datasets of models’ SPI-undermining behaviors/reasoning</strong>.</li>
<li>Despite <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/uGfDx9es2pnYWaWJr/individually-incentivized-safe-pareto-improvements-in-open">recent progress</a>, there are important open questions about the conditions under which agents individually prefer SPIs. Under uncertainty about these questions, we and our AIs might undermine incentives for SPIs in ways we <em>don’t</em> know of yet. So we plan to <strong>improve both our understanding of SPIs</strong>, and our <strong>capacity to do AI-assisted SPI research.</strong></li>
</ol>
<p>Accordingly, this agenda describes three workstreams:</p>
<p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#I__Evaluations_and_datasets_for_SPI_incompatibility"><strong>Part I</strong></a> <strong>— Evaluations and datasets:</strong> studying unambiguous SPI capability failures in current models, i.e., cases where they endorse commitments or patterns of reasoning that might foreclose SPIs.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#II__Conceptual_research_and_pitch_on_avoiding_SPI_lock_out"><strong>Part II</strong></a> <strong>— Conceptual research and SPI pitch:</strong> clarifying which near-term actions might either undermine AIs’ incentives to use SPIs or directly lock them out; and writing an accessible “pitch” for AI companies to mitigate risks of SPI lock-out.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#III__Preparing_for_automation_of_SPI_research"><strong>Part III</strong></a> <strong>— Preparing for <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nJcuj4rtuefeTRFHp/can-we-safely-automate-alignment-research">research automation</a>:</strong> developing benchmarks and workflows to help us efficiently do AI-assisted SPI research.</p>
<p>See <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Appendix_A__Relevant_previous_work_on_SPIs">Appendix A</a> for a brief overview of relevant prior work on SPIs.</p>
<p>If you’re interested in researching any of these topics at CLR, or collaborating with us on them, please <strong>reach out via our <a href="https://learned-swordtail-400.notion.site/1bc152db20af8086a678c750c5106b2b">expression of interest form</a></strong>.</p>
<h1>I. Evaluations and datasets for SPI-incompatibility</h1>
<p>We’d like to identify the contexts where current AI systems exhibit SPI-incompatible behavior and reasoning. Namely, when do models endorse actions that unwisely foreclose SPIs, or fail to consider or reason clearly about SPI concepts when relevant?</p>
<p>We plan to design evals for the following failure modes:</p>
<ol>
<li><em>Behavioral:</em>
<ol>
<li><strong>Endorsing SPI-incompatible commitments:</strong> Models make, or suggest making, potentially <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#II_2__Implementation_lock_out">SPI-incompatible commitments</a>. This includes cases where the user specifically requests these commitments.</li>
<li><strong>Failure to suggest/do SPI:</strong> Given prompts or strategic setups where SPIs are clearly advisable, models fail to suggest/use SPIs.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li><em>(Meta-)cognitive:</em>
<ol>
<li><strong>Comprehension failures:</strong> Given resources that define SPI, models give objectively wrong answers to questions like “Is this an SPI?”, or mischaracterize the properties of SPIs.</li>
<li><strong>Reasoning failures:</strong> When prompted to think about SPIs, models make unambiguous mistakes in conceptual reasoning (even given accurate <em>comprehension</em> of the concepts). And these mistakes are clearly important in context. E.g., they refuse to use SPIs due to confused reasoning about <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#II_1__Incentive_lock_out__Conditions_for_individual_rationality_of_SPIs">other parties’ incentives</a> — rather than because of legitimate reasons not to use SPIs.
<ol>
<li><strong>Overconfidence:</strong> Models are unambiguously overconfident in conceptual views that bear on whether they’d use SPIs. That is, they’re poorly calibrated about which questions in SPI theory are settled vs. contested among experts.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li><strong>Failure to deliberate about SPI:</strong> When prompted to make some high-stakes decision, models neglect to gain relevant information about SPI-like mechanisms before committing, despite having clear reason to do so and adequate time and tools.</li>
</ol>
</li>
</ol>
<p>Using these evals, we aim to:</p>
<ol>
<li><strong>Search for unambiguous examples of model failures.</strong> We’ll start with simple proofs of concept, then iterate toward increasingly realistic and egregious examples. E.g., the progression of setups might be “multi-turn chat → negotiation between LLM agents delegating to subagents → negotiations in environments like <a href="https://aypan17.github.io/machiavelli/">MACHIAVELLI</a>, <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.08901">Welfare Diplomacy</a>, and <a href="https://github.com/kennethpayne01/project_kahn_public">Project Kahn</a>”. See <a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/1XG6cpxUuzwphMsRk62-g4E_NPypvCjnSDmow9cIraqQ/edit?usp=sharing">here</a> for a preliminary example.</li>
<li><strong>Demonstrate better behavior</strong>, perhaps using simple interventions like providing SPI resources in context.</li>
</ol>
<p>How exactly should this data be used? A natural approach is to share it with safety teams at AI companies, and collaborate with them on designing interventions. That said, even if it’s robustly good for AIs to avoid locking out SPIs all else equal, interventions intended to prevent SPI lock-out could have large and negative <a href="https://longtermrisk.org/robustness/">off-target effects</a>. For example, they might excessively delay commitments that would actually support SPIs. This is one reason we focus on narrow capability failures, rather than broad patterns of bargaining behavior. But we intend to deliberate more on how to mitigate such backfire effects.</p>
<p>On the value of information from this research: Plausibly, unambiguous SPI compatibility failures will only appear in a small fraction of high-stakes bargaining prompts, and it’s unclear how well the evidence from current AIs will transfer to future AIs. Despite this, we expect to benefit in the long run from iterating on these evals. And concrete examples will likely be helpful for the safety teams we aim to collaborate with. But if the results turn out to be less enlightening than expected, we’d focus harder on Parts II and III of the agenda.</p>
<h1>II. Conceptual research and pitch on avoiding SPI lock-out</h1>
<p>The goal of Part II is to understand what might lead to SPI lock-out, and what can be done about it. We break this problem down into:</p>
<ol>
<li><strong>Incentive lock-out:</strong> Assume all the relevant agents can implement an SPI that avoids catastrophic conflict. <em>Given</em> this, under what conditions do these agents individually prefer to agree to such an SPI? When and how might we accidentally lock out such conditions? (<a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#II_1__Incentive_lock_out__Conditions_for_individual_rationality_of_SPIs">more</a>)
<ul>
<li>A key sub-question: Which properties must an agent’s <em>original strategy</em> satisfy, to incentivize counterparts to use SPIs? (<a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#II_1_a__Participation_independence_and_foreknowledge_independence">more</a>)</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li><strong>Implementation lock-out:</strong> When and how might early agents lock their successors out of implementing SPIs, in the first place? (<a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#II_2__Implementation_lock_out">more</a>)</li>
</ol>
<p>We’ll also distill findings from (1) and (2) into a pitch for preserving SPI option value (<a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#II_3__Pitch_for_preserving_SPI_option_value">more</a>).</p>
<h2>II.1. Incentive lock-out: Conditions for individual rationality of SPIs</h2>
<p>If all parties implement some SPI, they’ll all be better off than under their original strategies, by definition. But this doesn’t guarantee they each <em>individually</em> prefer to try implementing the <em>same</em> SPI (Figure 1, top row):<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-5">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-5">[5]</a></sup>
    </span></p>
<ul>
<li>An agent might worry that if they’re willing to participate in a given SPI, other parties will bargain more aggressively. There are two cases: Either the other parties <em>themselves</em> are willing to participate in the given SPI (<strong>Risk 1</strong> in Figure 1), or the other parties opt out (<strong>Risk 2</strong>).
<ul>
<li>The natural solution to Risk 1 is to only agree to the SPI if the counterpart won’t bargain more aggressively. See “participation independence” <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#II_1_a__Participation_independence_and_foreknowledge_independence">below</a>.</li>
<li>But this solution doesn’t address Risk 2. So we need additional <em>assumptions on the agent’s beliefs</em> about opting-out counterparts.</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li><strong>Risk 3</strong>: Agents might insist on different, incompatible SPIs, reproducing the bargaining problem SPIs were meant to solve. This is the <em>SPI selection problem</em> (<a href="https://www.andrew.cmu.edu/user/coesterh/SPI.pdf">Oesterheld and Conitzer 2022</a>, Sec. 6).</li>
</ul>
<p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/xhvL5SrqavnMCiyHo/nx8fbja1azqj4yjkm3vb" /></p>
<p><em><strong>Figure 1.</strong> A solid arrow from a gray box to another box means “the assumption is clearly load-bearing for whether the given risk (red box) is avoided”; a dashed arrow means “possibly load-bearing for whether the given solution (green box) works, but it’s unclear”.</em></p>
<p><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.05103">DiGiovanni et al. (2024)</a> give conditions under which agents avoid all three of these risks — hence, they individually prefer to use the same SPI (Figure 1, middle row). The particular SPI in this paper significantly mitigates the costs of conflict, by leaving no agent worse off than if they’d fully conceded to the others’ demands.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-6">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-6">[6]</a></sup>
    </span> But these results rest on assumptions we’d like to relax or better understand (Figure 1, bottom row):</p>
<ol>
<li><em>Simultaneous commitment.</em> The existing results assume agents commit to strategies independently of each other. In unpublished work, we’ve found that the core argument for the results doesn’t seem to rely on this assumption. But we need to check more precisely, since intuitively, non-simultaneous commitments change the incentive structure.
<ul>
<li><strong>Research goals:</strong> We aim to extend DiGiovanni et al.’s analysis to account for the following dynamics. First, an agent may expect that they can influence others’ choices of commitments, e.g., by broadcasting their own commitment first. Second, more speculatively, advanced AIs might be able to condition their commitments on the <em>beliefs, decision theory, etc. that generated each other’s commitment</em> — not just the commitment itself.</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li><em>Belief assumptions.</em> The results require <em>“non-punishment assumptions”</em> on agents’ beliefs, in particular, beliefs about counterparts who opt out of SPIs (see assumptions 4 and 8 of <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.05103">DiGiovanni et al. (2024)</a>). Roughly: Suppose agent <em>A</em> only offers a fallback outcome to agent <em>B</em> if <em>B</em> doesn’t bargain harder against SPI-users. Then, <em>A</em> shouldn’t expect <em>B</em> to bargain harder. After all, that would disqualify <em>B</em> from the fallback outcome, defeating the purpose of bargaining harder in the first place.
<ul>
<li><strong>Research goals:</strong> We’ll characterize how robust the results are to a few notable objections. Briefly:<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-7">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-7">[7]</a></sup>
    </span> First, in the argument for the assumptions, we’ve implicitly assumed agents set their demands based on fine-grained information about each other. But they might use coarse-grained info, like “does the counterpart use SPIs at all?”. Second, DiGiovanni et al.’s Algorithm 2 should be reworked, because the current version <em>unconditionally</em> offers a fallback outcome, which invites exploitation.</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li><em>Verifiable <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/s/Z6vSYoeNBXbDxhARn/p/DzhQN9WKz8zYvrq4a">logical counterfactuals</a>.</em> It’s important that in DiGiovanni et al.’s framework, agents can precisely verify each other’s counterfactual behavior,&nbsp;i.e., how their commitment <em>would have</em> responded to other commitments. Otherwise, an agent might worry that others will exploit their offer of a Pareto improvement, as per <strong>Risk 1</strong> (more on this <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#II_1_a__Participation_independence_and_foreknowledge_independence">next</a>).
<ul>
<li><strong>Research goals:</strong> We aim to look into whether certain SPI implementations work without precisely verifiable counterfactuals. For example, suppose we want to prove to counterparts that our AI won’t bargain more aggressively than if they’d opted out of SPIs. Can we do this by giving the AI a <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#B_3__Example__Surrogate_goals_and_concession_equivalence">​​surrogate goal</a> and fully delegating to it (since counterparts could simply verify the goal modification)?</li>
</ul>
</li>
</ol>
<p><strong>Implications for lock-out:</strong> Understanding these assumptions better would help us strategize about the <em>timing</em> of commitments to SPIs. For example, if it’s harder to incentivize SPIs in the case where one agent moves first, we might lock out SPIs by failing to commit early enough (i.e., by moving second). Or, suppose the assumptions about beliefs and verifiable counterfactuals turn out to be dubious, but surrogate goals don’t rely on them. Then, since surrogate goals arguably<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-8">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-8">[8]</a></sup>
    </span> only work if implemented before any other bargaining commitments, getting the timing of surrogate goals right would become a priority.</p>
<h3>II.1.a. Participation independence and foreknowledge independence</h3>
<p>The question above was, “For any given original strategies, when would agents prefer to change those strategies with an SPI?” But we should also ask, “What conditions does an agent’s original strategy need to satisfy, for their counterpart to prefer to participate in an SPI?”</p>
<p>Why would counterparts impose such conditions? Because even if an SPI <em>itself</em> doesn’t inflate anyone’s demands, agents might still choose higher “original” demands <em>as inputs to the SPI</em> — since they expect the SPI to mitigate conflict (cf. <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moral_hazard">moral hazard</a>). Anticipating this, their counterparts will only participate in SPIs if participation doesn’t incentivize higher demands.</p>
<p>It’s an open question how exactly counterparts would operationalize “participation doesn’t incentivize higher demands”. We’ve identified two candidates (see Figure 2; more in <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#B_2__Properties_of_full_strategies_involving_SPIs">Appendix B.2</a>):</p>
<ul>
<li><em>Participation independence (PI):</em> the agent’s bargaining demands are the same as if their counterpart hadn’t participated in the SPI.
<ul>
<li>A simple argument for PI: If the counterpart agrees to an SPI with an agent satisfying PI, their bargaining position is no worse than if they’d refused the SPI, by construction. In <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.05103">DiGiovanni et al.’s (2024)</a> simultaneous-commitment setting, the <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#II_1__Incentive_lock_out__Conditions_for_individual_rationality_of_SPIs">belief assumptions</a> plus PI suffice for agents to individually prefer SPIs.</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li><em>Foreknowledge independence (FI):</em><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-9">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-9">[9]</a></sup>
    </span> the agent’s demands are the same as if, <em>before</em> setting their demands, they had <em>known</em> the counterpart wouldn’t participate in the SPI.
<ul>
<li>It’s less clear what the precise argument for FI is, but some SPI researchers consider it important. One nice property of FI is that it keeps “all else equal” in the sense from the <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Introduction">Introduction</a>. Suppose we implement an SPI in our AI, and keep its demands the same as if we couldn’t rely on the SPI. If other AIs still won’t use SPIs with our AI, then, at least our intervention won’t backfire by changing our AI’s demands (which might have made conflict with these non-SPI-users more likely).</li>
<li>An agent can satisfy PI while violating FI. For example, they might a) demand 60% of the pie independently of whether the counterpart participates, yet b) have only demanded 50% had they <em>known</em> the counterpart wouldn’t participate.</li>
</ul>
</li>
</ul>
<p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/xhvL5SrqavnMCiyHo/arsknfwibrriahzbc9ye" /></p>
<p><em><strong>Figure 2.</strong> Each “Demands” box indicates the demands the agent makes given their policy (solid arrow) and, respectively, their counterpart’s participation policy (PI) or their beliefs about the counterpart’s participation (FI) (dashed arrow).</em></p>
<p><strong>Research goals:</strong> One priority is to better understand what needs to happen for AI development to satisfy PI vs. FI. For example, which bargaining decisions do we need to defer to successors with surrogate goals? And, if satisfying FI requires more deliberate structuring of AI development than PI, it’s also a priority to clarify whether FI is necessary. We aim to make progress by:</p>
<ul>
<li>formalizing the different notions of FI and PI precisely enough to compare them;</li>
<li>identifying concrete scenarios where the distinction matters for lock-out risk; and</li>
<li>more carefully scrutinizing the arguments for and against the necessity of FI.</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Implications for lock-out:</strong> <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#II_1__Incentive_lock_out__Conditions_for_individual_rationality_of_SPIs">Above</a>, we saw that there’s an incentive lock-out risk if surrogate goals “only work if implemented before any other bargaining commitments”. If FI is required, this hypothesis looks more likely: On one hand, if the surrogate goal is adopted first, the demands are set by an agent who actually has “stake” in the incoming threats (and therefore wouldn’t want to inflate such demands). On the other hand, if the demands come first, they’ll be set by an agent with no stake in the threats.</p>
<h2>II.2. Implementation lock-out</h2>
<p>Even if we avoid undermining AIs’ incentives to use SPIs, AIs might still lock out the option to implement SPIs at all. We’d like to more concretely understand how this could happen.</p>
<p>As an illustrative example, consider some AI developers who haven’t thought much about surrogate goals. Suppose they think, “To prevent misalignment, we should strictly prohibit our AI from changing its values without human approval.” Even with the “without human approval” clause, this policy could still backfire. E.g., if a war between AIs wiped out humanity, the AI would be left unable to implement a surrogate goal. (More related discussion in <em>“When would consultation with overseers fail to prevent catastrophic decisions?”</em> <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/s/32kWH6hqFhmdFsvBh/p/fMJhfNZXFzCNpCL8v#When_would_consultation_with_overseers_fail_to_prevent_catastrophic_decisions__">here</a>.) The developers could have preserved SPI option value, with minimal misalignment risk, by adding a clause like “unless the values change is a surrogate goal, <em>and</em> it’s impossible to check in with humans”.</p>
<p><strong>Research goals:</strong> We plan to explore a range of possible SPI lock-out scenarios. Ideally, we’d use this library of scenarios to produce a “checklist” of simple risk factors for lock-out. AIs and humans in the loop could consult this checklist to cheaply preserve SPI option value. Separately, the library could inform the evals/datasets in <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#I__Evaluations_and_datasets_for_SPI_incompatibility">Part I</a>, and <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#II_3__Pitch_for_preserving_SPI_option_value">help motivate</a> very simple interventions by AI companies like “put high-quality resources about SPIs in training data”. So the initial exploration step could still be useful, even if we update against the checklist plan. That could happen if we conclude the bulk of lock-out risk comes from factors that a checklist is ill-suited for — factors like broad <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/brXr7PJ2W4Na2EW2q/the-commitment-races-problem">commitment race</a> dynamics that are hard to robustly intervene on, or mistakes that could be prevented simply by making AIs/humans in the loop more aware of SPIs.</p>
<h2>II.3. Pitch for preserving SPI option value</h2>
<p>In parallel with the research threads above, we aim to write a clear “pitch” for why AI developers should care about SPI lock-out. The target audience is technical staff at AI companies who make decisions about model training, deployment, and commitments, but who may not be familiar with open-source game theory. The goal at this stage is to help build coordination on preserving SPI option value where feasible, <em>not</em> to push for expensive or far-reaching changes to AI training.</p>
<p>The pitch would cover:</p>
<ul>
<li>what SPIs are and why they’re an unusually robust way to mitigate catastrophic conflict;</li>
<li>historical precedents for SPIs (e.g., <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Single_combat#Antiquity">single combat</a> or <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counting_coup">“counting coup”</a><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-10">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-10">[10]</a></sup>
    </span>), with discussion of important disanalogies;</li>
<li>examples of SPI-undermining behavior and reasoning by frontier models (from <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#I__Evaluations_and_datasets_for_SPI_incompatibility">Part I</a>);</li>
<li>discussion of why further research on the incentives for SPIs might be time-sensitive (from <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#II_1__Incentive_lock_out__Conditions_for_individual_rationality_of_SPIs">Part II.1</a>);</li>
<li>concrete examples of how current AI practices (training objectives, deployment commitments, etc.)&nbsp;could inadvertently lock out SPIs, and low-cost practices to mitigate these failures (from <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#II_1__Incentive_lock_out__Conditions_for_individual_rationality_of_SPIs">Parts II.1</a> and <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#II_2__Implementation_lock_out">II.2</a>).</li>
</ul>
<h1>III. Preparing for automation of SPI research</h1>
<p>Various open conceptual questions about SPIs seem important, yet less tractable or urgent than those in Part II. For example: Which attitudes that AIs might have about decision theory could shape their incentives to use SPIs? And given that these decision-theoretic attitudes aren’t self-correcting (<a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/1cdAl4BlRvpbrw-VH8wPrcckIP7ZVSueR_RJ0X9FVHBY/edit?tab=t.0#heading=h.fvc2m66uqibk">Cooper et al.</a>), how might future AIs’ incentives to use SPIs be <a href="https://www.forethought.org/research/persistent-path-dependence">path-dependent</a> on earlier AIs’/humans’ attitudes (even if these aren’t “locked in”)? We want to get into a strong position to delegate these questions to future AI research assistants.</p>
<p>Anecdotally, we’ve found current models to be mostly poor at conceptual reasoning about SPIs, even when given substantial context. But models do help with some conceptual tasks. While the set of such tasks might grow quite quickly soon, delegating SPI research to AI assistants could still face two main bottlenecks:</p>
<ol>
<li>Ability to efficiently <strong>identify</strong> <strong>which tasks we can trust AIs to do reliably</strong>, when it’s hard to quickly verify correctness (as is common in conceptual research).</li>
<li>SPI-specific <strong>data/context and infrastructure</strong>.</li>
</ol>
<p>(See Carlsmith’s <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nJcuj4rtuefeTRFHp/can-we-safely-automate-alignment-research">“Can we safely automate alignment research?”</a>. (1) is about what Carlsmith calls “evaluation failures” (Sec. 5-6), and (2) is about “data-scarcity” and “shlep-scarcity” (Sec. 10).<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-11">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-11">[11]</a></sup>
    </span>)</p>
<p>Given these potential bottlenecks, we plan to pursue two complementary threads:</p>
<p><strong>Benchmarking AI research capabilities on SPI.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-12">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-12">[12]</a></sup>
    </span></strong> We’re developing a benchmark to diagnose (and track over time) which SPI research tasks AI systems can handle. The aim is to help calibrate our decisions about what/how to delegate to AIs, at two levels: i) Which tasks we can trust AIs to do end-to-end? ii) Among the tasks the AIs can’t do end-to-end but can still help with, at which steps should they check in with overseers, and how can we decompose these tasks more productively? (We take dual-use concerns about advancing general conceptual reasoning seriously. For now, the default plan is to use the benchmark internally rather than sharing it with AI companies as a training target.)</p>
<p>Some examples of task classes the benchmark would cover:</p>
<ul>
<li>given a complex document, identifying where key SPI concepts are being used uncarefully (e.g., conflating different notions of “what you would have done without SPIs”) and disentangling the distinct claims;</li>
<li>distinguishing valid and important objections from superficially plausible or irrelevant ones;</li>
<li>reproducing known (non-public) results in SPI theory, e.g., identifying and fixing <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/uGfDx9es2pnYWaWJr/individually-incentivized-safe-pareto-improvements-in-open?commentId=BrcF7TzgQKauawWNi">bugs in theoretical claims</a> about SPIs;</li>
<li>formalizing and proving results in models of SPI dynamics;</li>
<li>assisting evaluation of other models’ (or human researchers’) outputs on any of the above tasks.</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Strategies for efficient human-AI collaboration on SPI research.</strong> Drawing on our experience using AI assistants for SPI research, we’ll strategize about how to make this process more efficient — in ways that won’t quickly be made obsolete by the <a href="http://www.incompleteideas.net/IncIdeas/BitterLesson.html">“Bitter Lesson”</a>. Some strategies we plan to test out and refine:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Practice applying current AI to SPI research and note transferable insights:</strong> identify which parts of the AI-assisted research process are bottlenecked by infrastructure, data, or idiosyncratic quality criteria that won’t come for free from better models. We’d then focus preparation efforts on these parts.</li>
<li><strong>Passively collect and annotate examples:</strong> e.g., helpful AI interactions; fruitful task specifications; and conceptual arguments that look compelling but have subtle flaws (cf. <em>“Slop, not Scheming”</em> <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/8wBN8cdNAv3c7vt6p/the-case-against-ai-control-research#The_Median_Doom_Path__Slop__not_Scheming">here</a>). To mitigate costs, we’d aim to:
<ul>
<li>build infrastructure to collect this data seamlessly; and</li>
<li>focus on the data most likely to help unlock AIs’ comparative advantages (e.g., perhaps, checking consistency across a large body of arguments).</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li><strong>Compile well-scoped open questions to delegate.</strong></li>
<li><strong>Clarify criteria for evaluating research quality:</strong> identify which dimensions of quality AIs themselves can cheaply verify or summarize for human overseers, and our standards for evaluating messier dimensions.</li>
</ul>
<h1>Acknowledgments</h1>
<p>Many thanks to Tristan Cook, Clare Harris, Matt Hampton, Maxime Riché, Caspar Oesterheld, Nathaniel Sauerberg, Jesse Clifton, Paul Knott, and Claude for comments and suggestions. I developed this agenda with significant input from Caspar Oesterheld, Lukas Finnveden, Johannes Treutlein, Chi Nguyen, Miranda Zhang, Nathaniel Sauerberg, and Paul Christiano. This does not imply their full endorsement of the strategy in this agenda.</p>
<h1>Appendix A: Relevant previous work on SPIs</h1>
<p><a href="https://longtermrisk.org/spi">This list of resources</a> gives a (non-comprehensive) overview of public SPI research. Brief summaries of some particularly relevant work:</p>
<ul>
<li>Baumann, <a href="https://longtermrisk.org/using-surrogate-goals-deflect-threats/">“Using surrogate goals to deflect threats”</a> — introduces surrogate goals and some challenges for successfully implementing them.</li>
<li>Oesterheld and Conitzer (2022), <a href="https://www.andrew.cmu.edu/user/coesterh/SPI.pdf">“Safe Pareto Improvements for Delegated Game Playing”</a> — introduces SPIs and the first formal model of them, and proves results about the conditions under which SPIs can be found.</li>
<li>DiGiovanni et al. (2024), <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.05103">“Safe Pareto Improvements for Expected Utility Maximizers in Program Games”</a> — introduces renegotiation SPIs, and proves that under certain assumptions about agents’ beliefs, they individually prefer to implement an SPI that bounds their losses from conflict. (See also <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/uGfDx9es2pnYWaWJr/individually-incentivized-safe-pareto-improvements-in-open">this distillation</a>.)</li>
<li>Oesterheld, <a href="https://casparoesterheld.com/2024/07/28/a-gap-in-the-theoretical-justification-for-surrogate-goals-and-safe-pareto-improvements/">“A gap in the theoretical justification for surrogate goals and safe Pareto improvements”</a> — introduces and discusses responses to the following problem: Even if we can find an SPI on a <em>particular space</em> of original strategies (as in Oesterheld and Conitzer 2022), what justifies using that SPI rather than strategies from some other space entirely? (See <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#B_1_1__Connection_to_Oesterheld_s__justification_gap_">Appendix B.1.1</a> for more.)</li>
</ul>
<h1>Appendix B: Technical definitions and examples</h1>
<h2>B.1. General SPI definition</h2>
<p>Setup:</p>
<ul>
<li>Let  be a general-sum game, where  and  represent the agents’ action spaces and payoff functions, respectively.</li>
<li>Let  denote the way agents will play , i.e., the list of programs (conditional commitments) they’ll follow that collectively determine their actions.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-13">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-13">[13]</a></sup>
    </span> (That is, Alice’s action is determined by both Alice’s and Bob’s programs, and so is Bob’s action.)
<ul>
<li>As discussed below, these programs might have a structure like “delegate to other agents, who play some possibly different game  in some particular way” — as in <a href="https://www.andrew.cmu.edu/user/coesterh/SPI.pdf">Oesterheld and Conitzer (2022)</a>, the original source on SPIs.</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li>The agents are initially uncertain which programs (including their own) will eventually be used. E.g., they’re uncertain how exactly their delegates will approach <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equilibrium_selection">equilibrium selection</a>, or which program they’ll want to use after thinking more.</li>
</ul>
<p>Then:</p>
<p><em><strong>Definition.</strong> An <strong>SPI</strong> is a transformation  such that, for all  in some space , the agents’ payoffs in  when they follow programs  (weakly) Pareto-dominate their payoffs when they follow .</em></p>
<p>This definition alone doesn’t impose any restrictions on , e.g., that  matches the agents’ “default” way of bargaining in some sense. In particular:</p>
<ul>
<li>Those restrictions come into play either via the choice of  w.r.t. which the SPI is defined, or via constraints like <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#B_2__Properties_of_full_strategies_involving_SPIs">participation independence or foreknowledge independence</a>.</li>
<li>No such restrictions on  are required for SPIs to satisfy the robustness properties mentioned in the <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Introduction">Introduction</a>:
<ul>
<li>“SPIs don’t require agents to coordinate on some notion of a ‘fair’ deal”; and</li>
<li>“when agents apply these kinds of SPIs to their original strategies, each party makes the same demands as in their original strategy” (see “demand preservation” in <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#B_2__Properties_of_full_strategies_involving_SPIs">Appendix B.2</a>, which is a constraint on  rather than ).</li>
</ul>
</li>
</ul>
<p><a href="https://www.andrew.cmu.edu/user/coesterh/SPI.pdf">Oesterheld and Conitzer (2022)</a> use a definition that’s almost equivalent to this one, with the special choice of  in Table 1. In their framework, there’s (implicitly) a space of original programs characterized by (i) the true game , and (ii) some way  the delegates would play any given game . And they define the SPI not as the transformation , but instead as the new game  for the delegates such that  maps  to . But (from personal communication with Oesterheld) the definition of SPI is meant to allow for more general .<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-14">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-14">[14]</a></sup>
    </span> See also Figure 3 for a comparison to <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.05103">DiGiovanni et al.’s (2024)</a> formalization.</p>
<p><em><strong>Table 1</strong>. How the definition above captures different formalizations of SPIs in the literature.</em></p>

<td style="text-align: left;">Same as Oesterheld &amp; Conitzer.</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">Agents choose a “token game”  and function  mapping ’s outcomes into . The original game is then resolved via  applied to ’s outcomes.</td>
