<p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/xhvL5SrqavnMCiyHo/t05dia0xg9qfitaaklbs" /></p>
<p><em><strong>Figure 3.</strong></em></p>
<h3>B.1.1. Connection to Oesterheld’s “justification gap”</h3>
<p>Here’s how we might state the problem raised by Oesterheld’s <a href="https://casparoesterheld.com/2024/07/28/a-gap-in-the-theoretical-justification-for-surrogate-goals-and-safe-pareto-improvements/">“A gap in the theoretical justification for surrogate goals and safe Pareto improvements”</a>, in the formalism above.</p>
<p>Consider the original space of programs  in Oesterheld and Conitzer’s framework. The delegates can play the game in an arbitrary way, subject to the mild Assumptions 1 and 2. But it’s assumed that in , the game they play is the true game . So, take some SPI <em>with respect to this space</em> , a transformation from  to . By definition, this transformation makes all agents better off for all . But it’s not guaranteed that for all  <em>and</em> all , all agents are better off under  than under .</p>
<p>This suggests one way to bridge the justification gap: find an  that’s an SPI with respect to <em>any arbitrary program space </em>, as <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.05103">DiGiovanni et al. (2024)</a> aims to do. Cf. Oesterheld’s discussion of “decision factorization” in the justification gap post.</p>
<p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/xhvL5SrqavnMCiyHo/kkgxzdlzt5istgwvtovq" /></p>
<p><em><strong>Figure 4.</strong></em></p>
<h2>B.2. Properties of full strategies involving SPIs</h2>
<p><em>(These are working formalizations of participation independence and foreknowledge independence. “Foreknowledge independence” and “demand preservation” are working terminology. We’re not highly confident that we’ll endorse these formalizations/terminology after more thought.)</em></p>
<p>If  is an SPI and  are the programs the agents in fact apply  to, call  the agents’ <strong>full strategy</strong>. It’s helpful to distinguish an SPI from the full strategy, because in general agents will only individually prefer to agree to some SPI <em>conditional</em> on the input programs satisfying certain restrictions.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#II_1_a__Participation_independence_and_foreknowledge_independence">Participation independence and foreknowledge independence</a>, as well as the “preserving bargaining power” property discussed in the <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Introduction">Introduction</a>, are properties of full strategies. These can be defined as follows.</p>
<p>Setup:</p>
<ul>
<li>For any program , let  represent the “demands” made by . This isn’t fully precise, but as an example: For a renegotiation program  as in the pseudocode in <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#B_4__Example__Renegotiation">Appendix B.4</a>,  my_base_strategy.</li>
<li>Given a full strategy , let:
<ul>
<li> be the program that agent  would have chosen had each other agent  used the program , rather than ;</li>
<li> be the program that agent  would have chosen had  <em>believed</em> each other agent  would use the program , rather than .</li>
</ul>
</li>
</ul>
<p>Then:</p>
<p><em><strong>Definition</strong>. A full strategy  is:</em></p>
<ul>
<li><em><strong>demand-preserving</strong> if for each agent </em>, <em>.</em></li>
<li><em><strong>participation-independent</strong> if it’s demand-preserving and for each agent , .</em></li>
<li><em><strong>foreknowledge-independent</strong> if it’s demand-preserving and for each agent , .</em></li>
</ul>
<p>Commentary on these definitions:</p>
<ul>
<li>Demand preservation is equivalent to this property informally stated in the <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Introduction">Introduction</a>: “When agents apply these kinds of SPIs to their original strategies, each party makes the same demands as in their original strategy.”</li>
<li>For each of PI and FI, the two conditions correspond to two different levels at which “your demands are the same as if [PI counterfactual or FI counterfactual]”. That is:
<ul>
<li> or , respectively, formalizes this property at the level of the agent’s <em>choice of the program the SPI is applied to</em>.</li>
<li><em>Given</em> that  or  holds, demand preservation formalizes this property at the level of the <em>output</em> of the fixed program . This is because the transformation  is applied to all the agents’ programs. Thus, if (agent  believed that) agent  wouldn’t participate in , neither would  — so the demands made by ’s program would be the same as under ’s original program.</li>
</ul>
</li>
</ul>
<p><em>Example:</em> In <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.05103">DiGiovanni et al.’s (2024)</a> setting, suppose agents use the SPI  given by Proposition 1 (or Proposition 4). Then participation independence is satisfied for any input program profile , because:</p>
<ul>
<li>agents choose programs independently of each other, so it’s immediate that ;</li>
<li>by construction, renegotiation programs satisfy . Compare lines 3 and 10 of the pseudocode in <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#B_4__Example__Renegotiation">Appendix B.4</a>.</li>
</ul>
<h2>B.3. Example: Surrogate goals and concession equivalence</h2>
<p><em>(This section is based on previous joint work with Mia Taylor, Nathaniel Sauerberg, Julian Stastny, and Jesse Clifton.)</em></p>
<p>One key example of an SPI is a surrogate goal. More precisely, the (approximate) SPI here is, “<em>A</em> adopts a surrogate goal, and <em>B</em> threatens the surrogate goal whenever an executed surrogate threat would be less costly for <em>B</em> than the default threat”. (More below on why this is an SPI.)</p>
<p>An agent doesn’t need to broadly modify its preferences in order to implement an SPI of this form, though. We can generalize the idea of surrogate goals as follows:</p>
<ul>
<li>A <strong>surrogate threat</strong> is a claim by a threatener that they will cause some outcome , at some cost to themselves but no cost to the target’s original goal, if the target doesn’t concede to some demand.</li>
<li>A target’s bargaining policy is <strong>-concession-equivalent</strong> to its policy for responding to surrogate threats if, for any demand, the target is equally likely to concede to the demand given (i) a surrogate threat as given (ii) an equally credible threat against the target’s original goal (“OG threat”), when executing the OG threat is  times as costly to the threatener as executing the surrogate threat.
<ul>
<li>In cases where concession equivalence isn’t perfectly credible, choosing a cost ratio  can allow targets to compensate threateners for taking the risk of making a surrogate threat, thereby still allowing for threat deflection.</li>
<li>For brevity, we’ll often just say that a target’s policy is “-concession-equivalent” if it has this property, and <strong>“concession-equivalent”</strong> if  for some very small .</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li>A <strong>surrogate goal</strong> is a modification to an agent’s preferences in which they terminally disprefer the outcome .</li>
</ul>
<p>Why is adoption of a concession-equivalent policy an SPI? Suppose — holding all else fixed — <em>A</em> becomes just as likely to concede to a surrogate threat that would give <em>B</em> utility  if executed, as to an OG threat that would give <em>B</em> utility  if executed. Then <em>B</em> would rather make a surrogate threat than an OG threat. So any executed threats would be less bad for both parties, but neither party would have an incentive to change how much they demand. (Except, perhaps, a very small increase in <em>B</em>’s demands in proportion to the difference in disutility of executing an OG threat vs. surrogate threat.) Both parties are then better off overall, no matter how much they demand.</p>
<p>See also <a href="https://www.andrew.cmu.edu/user/coesterh/SPI.pdf">Oesterheld and Conitzer’s (2022)</a> “Demand Game” (Table 1), as an example of something like a <em>bilateral</em> surrogate goal.</p>
<h2>B.4. Example: Renegotiation</h2>
<p>A <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.05103"><strong>renegotiation</strong></a> <strong>program</strong> is a <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#B_1__General_SPI_definition">program</a> structured like: <em>“If they don’t use a renegotiation program, act according to program . Otherwise, still act according to , except: if we get into conflict, propose some Pareto improvement(s) and take it if our proposals match.”</em> In pseudocode (see also Algorithms 1 and 2 of <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.05103">DiGiovanni et al. (2024)</a>):</p>
<p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/xhvL5SrqavnMCiyHo/y9zcpt1kaqicqw2d0ufr" /></p>
<p>For example, suppose agents <em>A</em> and <em>B</em> are negotiating over what values to instill in a successor agent. If they fail to reach an agreement, they’ll each attempt to take over. They simultaneously submit programs for the negotiation to some centralized server. Before they consider the possibility of SPIs, they’re inclined to choose these programs, respectively:</p>
<ul>
<li><em>A: </em> = <em>“Demand 50% of the share of the ASI’s values no matter what”;</em></li>
<li><em>B: </em> = <em>“Demand 80%, and trigger a doomsday device if they refuse.”</em></li>
</ul>
<p>Since the demands selected by these programs would be incompatible, the outcome would be “<em>B</em> triggers a doomsday device”. In this scenario, the agents’ corresponding renegotiation programs might be:</p>
<ul>
<li><em>A: </em> = <em>“<strong>If they don’t use a renegotiation program</strong>, demand 50% of the share of the ASI’s values. <strong>Otherwise, demand 50%; if they refuse, propose ‘attempt takeover, without any doomsday devices’</strong>.”</em></li>
<li><em>B: </em> = <em>“<strong>If they don’t use a renegotiation program</strong>, demand 80% and trigger a doomsday device if they refuse. <strong>Otherwise, demand 80%; if they refuse: propose ‘attempt takeover, without any doomsday devices’</strong>.”</em></li>
</ul>
<p>(Here, the Pareto improvement is to the outcome “both agents attempt takeover, without any doomsday devices”, rather than “<em>B</em> triggers a doomsday device”. Both here and in the <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#B_3__Example__Surrogate_goals_and_concession_equivalence">surrogate goals example</a>, we’re setting aside the additional conditions necessary for these SPIs to be individually preferable. See <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#II_1__Incentive_lock_out__Conditions_for_individual_rationality_of_SPIs">Part II.1</a> and <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#B_2__Properties_of_full_strategies_involving_SPIs">Appendix B.2</a> for more. But note one such condition in this example:  and  demand 50% and 80%, respectively, <em>regardless</em> of whether the other program is a renegotiation program. See “demand preservation” in Appendix B.2.)</p>
<p>See Macé et al., <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/uGfDx9es2pnYWaWJr/individually-incentivized-safe-pareto-improvements-in-open">“Individually incentivized safe Pareto improvements in open-source bargaining”</a>, for more discussion of how a special class of renegotiation programs can partially resolve the SPI selection problem.</p>
<ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-1"><p>“Commitments” are meant to include modifications to one’s decision theory or values/preferences. It has been argued (<a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/PcfHSSAMNFMgdqFyB/can-you-control-the-past#IX__What_would_you_have_wanted_yourself_to_commit_to_">example</a>) that decision theories like updateless decision theory (UDT) can sidestep the need for “commitments” in the usual sense. We’ll set this question aside here, and treat the resolution to make one’s <em>future</em> decisions according to UDT as a commitment in itself. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-1">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-2"><p>We might wonder: We’ve assumed the AIs are capable of conditional commitments. So, suppose each AI could commit to only demand 60% <em>unless</em> they verify that the other AI has made an incompatible commitment. Would this solve the problem? Not necessarily, because the AIs might reason, “If they see that I’ll revoke my commitment conditional on incompatible demands, they’ll exploit this by making high demands. So I should stick with my unconditional commitment.” <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-2">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-3"><p>However, see <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#II_1__Incentive_lock_out__Conditions_for_individual_rationality_of_SPIs">Part II.1</a> for discussion of the “SPI selection problem”. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-3">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-4"><p>(H/t Caspar Oesterheld and Nathaniel Sauerberg:) Another important reason is that even if SPIs don’t get locked out, they might not be implemented early enough, before conflicts break out. We put less emphasis on this consideration in this agenda, because <em>avoiding locking out</em> SPIs is a less controversial ask than actively prioritizing implementing SPIs. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-4">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-5"><p>These gaps are related to, but importantly distinct from, the <a href="https://casparoesterheld.com/2024/07/28/a-gap-in-the-theoretical-justification-for-surrogate-goals-and-safe-pareto-improvements/">“SPI justification gap”</a> discussed by Oesterheld. Oesterheld’s question is: Suppose we have some SPI that makes everyone better off relative to particular “default” strategies — <em>not</em> necessarily relative to any possible original strategies. If so, why would agents use the SPI-transformed strategies, rather than some alternatives to both the default strategies <em>and</em> SPI transformations of them? More in <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#B_1_1__Connection_to_Oesterheld_s__justification_gap_">Appendix B.1.1</a>. By contrast, the question here is: Suppose we have an SPI that is <em>ex post</em> better for everyone relative to <em>any</em> original strategies. (So there is no privileged “default”.) Then, when do agents prefer to implement this SPI <em>ex ante</em>, rather than use their original strategies? <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-5">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-6"><p>See also <a href="http://www.lesswrong.com/posts/uGfDx9es2pnYWaWJr/individually-incentivized-safe-pareto-improvements-in-open">this distillation</a>. The rough intuition for the result is: If you’re (only) willing to fall back to Pareto improvements that aren’t better for the other agent than conceding 100%, you don’t give them perverse incentives (cf. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/z2YwmzuT7nWx62Kfh/cooperating-with-agents-with-different-ideas-of-fairness">Yudkowsky</a>). And if you <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/uGfDx9es2pnYWaWJr/individually-incentivized-safe-pareto-improvements-in-open#Conditional_set_valued_renegotiation">offer a <em>set</em> of possible Pareto improvements</a> with this property, you can coordinate on an SPI despite the SPI selection problem. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-6">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-7"><p>In more detail, respectively: (1) (H/t James Faville and Lukas Finnveden:) Agents might be incentivized to condition their demands on coarse-grained proxies about their counterparts, because they worry about being exploited if they use fine-grained information (cf. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/g8HHKaWENEbqh2mgK/updatelessness-doesn-t-solve-most-problems-1#A_fundamental_trade_off">Soto</a>). And an agent who opts out of SPIs might bargain more aggressively against SPI-participating agents, based on such proxies. (2) (H/t Lukas Finnveden:) Roughly, the “PMP-extension” of Algorithm 2 from <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.05103">DiGiovanni et al. (2024)</a> offers a fallback outcome to an agent willing to use <em>any</em> <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/uGfDx9es2pnYWaWJr/individually-incentivized-safe-pareto-improvements-in-open#Conditional_set_valued_renegotiation">“conditional set-valued renegotiation”</a> algorithm. This means that a counterpart has little to lose by <em>renegotiating more aggressively</em> against this algorithm. (It appears straightforward to avoid this problem by making the offer conditional, but we need to confirm this makes sense formally — see <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/uGfDx9es2pnYWaWJr/individually-incentivized-safe-pareto-improvements-in-open?commentId=BrcF7TzgQKauawWNi">this comment</a>.) More precisely, the “fallback outcome” is the <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/uGfDx9es2pnYWaWJr/individually-incentivized-safe-pareto-improvements-in-open#The_Pareto_meet_minimum_bound">“Pareto meet minimum”</a>. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-7">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-8"><p>See, e.g., <a href="https://casparoesterheld.com/2024/07/28/a-gap-in-the-theoretical-justification-for-surrogate-goals-and-safe-pareto-improvements/">Oesterheld</a> (section <em>“Solution idea 2: Decision factorization”</em>): “[I]n the surrogate goal story, it’s important to first adopt surrogate goals and only then decide whether to make other commitments.” <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-8">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-9"><p>Working terminology. Cf. <a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/K4FrKRTrmyxrw5Dip/formalizing-objections-against-surrogate-goals">Kovarik</a> (section <em>“Illustrating our Main Objection: Unrealistic Framing”</em>); and <a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/K4FrKRTrmyxrw5Dip/formalizing-objections-against-surrogate-goals?commentId=e6qq8JH5TLwJQGMxH">Oesterheld</a>: “If in 20 years I instruct an AI to manage my resources, it would be problematic if in the meantime I make tons of decisions (e.g., about how to train my AI systems) differently based on my knowledge that I will use surrogate goals anyway.” The concept of foreknowledge independence was also inspired by <a href="https://longtermrisk.org/using-surrogate-goals-deflect-threats/#Threatener-neutrality">Baumann’s</a> notion of “threatener-neutrality”. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-9">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-10"><p>Thanks to Jesse Clifton and Carl Shulman for these examples. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-10">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-11"><p>In the context of SPI research, we’re not too concerned about a third problem Carlsmith discusses: <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nJcuj4rtuefeTRFHp/can-we-safely-automate-alignment-research#9__What_about_scheming_">deliberate sabotage by “scheming” AIs</a>. This is because SPIs are designed to make all parties better off, so a misaligned AI doesn’t clearly have an incentive to sabotage SPI research. But we’ll aim to be mindful of sabotage risks as well. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-11">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-12"><p>See also <a href="https://www.andrew.cmu.edu/user/coesterh/LMCA_dataset.pdf">Oesterheld et al. (2026)</a> and <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.10588">Oesterheld et al. (2025)</a> for related datasets of rated conceptual arguments and decision theory reasoning, respectively. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-12">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-13"><p><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.05103">DiGiovanni et al. (2024)</a>, Sec. 3.1, gives a more precise definition of programs. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-13">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-14"><p>See also <a href="https://www.andrew.cmu.edu/user/coesterh/SPI.pdf">Oesterheld and Conitzer (2022)</a>, p. 30: “In principle, Theorem 3 does not hinge on Π(Γ) and Π(Γs) resulting from playing games. An analogous result holds for any random variables over A and As. In particular, this means that Theorem 3 applies also if the representatives [i.e., delegates] receive other kinds of instructions.” <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-14">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-15"><p>In the <em>formalism</em> of <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.05103">DiGiovanni et al. (2024)</a>, there is no separate stage where agents choose a transformation <strong>f</strong> before choosing programs from the new space of programs. Agents simply choose programs directly. But, for the purposes of modeling SPIs and comparing the framework of DiGiovanni et al. with that of <a href="https://www.andrew.cmu.edu/user/coesterh/SPI.pdf">Oesterheld and Conitzer (2022)</a>, it’s helpful to use the framing in Table 1. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-BXJeuRMHDaLcKdCqw-15">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
</ol>
<br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/YAie7SxrB28ZksLvE/clr-s-safe-pareto-improvements-research-agenda-1#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Quality Matters Most When Stakes are Highest](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/GNjDC6jtjr2iiE45i/quality-matters-most-when-stakes-are-highest)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span>Or</span></i><span>, </span><i><span>the end of the world is no excuse for sloppy work</span></i></p><p><span>One morning when I was nine, my dad called me over to his computer. He wanted to show me this amazing Korean scientist who had managed to clone stem cells, and who was developing treatments to let people with spinal cord injuries – people like my dad – walk again on their own two legs.</span></p><p><span>I don't remember exactly what he said next, or what I said back. I have a sense that I was excited too, and that I was upset when I learned the United States had banned this kind of research.</span></p><p><span>Unfortunately, his research didn’t pan out. No such treatment arrived. My dad still walks on crutches.</span></p><p><span>Years later, I learned that the scientist, </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hwang_Woo-suk" rel="noreferrer"><span>Hwang Woo-Suk</span></a><span>, had been exposed as a fraud.</span></p><hr /><p><span>In 2004, Hwang published a paper in Science claiming that his team had cloned a human embryo and derived stem cells from it (the first time anyone had done this). A year later, in 2005, he published a second paper claiming that they managed to repeat this feat </span><i><span>eleven more</span></i><span> times, producing 11 patient-specific stem cell lines for patients with type 1 diabetes, </span><a href="https://my.clevelandclinic.org/health/diseases/25195-hypogammaglobulinemia"><span>congenital hypogammaglobulinemia</span></a><span> (a rare immune disorder), and spinal cord injuries. This was the result that, if true, would have helped my dad.</span></p><p><span>None of this was real. The 2004 cell line did exist, but was not a clone; investigators concluded that it was an unfertilized egg that had spontaneously started dividing. The 2005 cell lines did not exist at all; investigators later found that the data reported for all eleven lines had been fabricated from just two samples, and the DNA in those two samples did not match the patients they had supposedly been derived from.</span></p><hr /><p><span>My dad was not the only person Hwang had given hope to. On July 31st, 2005, Hwang had appeared on a Korean TV show.  The dance duo Clon had just performed; one of its members, Kang Won-rae, had been paralyzed from the waist down in a motorcycle accident five years earlier, and had performed in his wheelchair. Hwang walked onto the stage and told a national audience, with tears in his eyes, that he hoped “for a day that Kang will get up and perform magnificently as he did in the past” – a day that was coming soon. He made similar promises to other patients and their families.</span></p><hr /><p><span>I don't think Hwang was a monster who set out to commit fraud for international acclaim. I think he was a capable scientist with real results. (Some of his lab’s cloned animals were almost certainly real clones, including the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snuppy"><span>world’s first cloned dog Snuppy</span></a><span>.) But over time,  he repeatedly took what he felt was his only option.</span></p><p><span>The 2004 paper may have started as a real mistake; it’s possible his team genuinely thought the parthenogenetic egg was a clone. But by 2005, with a nation watching and a Nobel on the table and a paralyzed pop star looking at him on live television, there was no version of "actually, we can't do this yet" that he could bring himself to say. So he didn't say it.</span></p><p><span>The way in which Hwang began his downward spiral is what sticks out most to me. He started out a good scientist, with good results and an important field of study. But with tens of millions of dollars of funding, thousands of adoring fans, and all the letters written to him by hopeful patients and their families, Hwang likely felt the weight of the world on his shoulders. He had to do what he had to do, in order to not let them down.</span></p><hr /><p><span>I work in AI safety. Many of the people I work with believe (and I believe) that the next decade will substantially determine whether and how humanity gets through this century. The stakes are literally astronomical and existential, and the timelines may be short.</span></p><p><span>That is the weight we carry.</span><span> And I worry that when push comes to shove, our scientific standards will slip (or are slipping) in order to not let other people down.</span></p><p><span>For example, wouldn’t it be the right choice to just accept the code written by Claude, without reading it carefully? We don’t have much time left, and we need to figure out how to do interpretability, or monitoring, or how to align models with personas, and so forth.</span></p><p><span>Why investigate that note of confusion about the new result you saw? Surely with the stakes involved, it’s important to push forward, rather than question every assumption we have?</span></p><p><span>Why question your interpretability tools, when they seem to produce results that make sense, and let you steer the models to produce other results that seem to make sense? Why flag the failed eval run with somewhat suspicious results, when the deadline for model release is coming soon, and evaluation setups are famously finicky and buggy anyways? Why not simplify away some of the nuance of your paper’s results, when doing so would let it reach a much larger audience?</span></p><p><span>I worry that it’s tempting for us to take the expedient choice and let our standards slip, precisely because the stakes are so high. But it is precisely because the stakes are so high, with all the real people who will be affected by the outcome, that we need to be vigilant.</span></p><p><span>Yes, timelines may be short and we may not have time to do all the research that we want.</span><span> But slipping up and producing misleading or wrong research will only hurt, not help. And if we need to say "actually, we can't do that yet", then we should say as much. </span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/GNjDC6jtjr2iiE45i/quality-matters-most-when-stakes-are-highest#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [If a room feels off the lighting is probably too "spiky" or too blue](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/dWib7qinqymfxevE4/if-a-room-feels-off-the-lighting-is-probably-too-spiky-or)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>I've designed a few spaces people seem to really love, most widely known </span><a href="http://lighthaven.space/" rel="noreferrer"><span>Lighthaven</span></a><span>.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref333l30t8x95"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn333l30t8x95">[1]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>Most people have surprisingly bad introspective access into why a space makes them feel certain things. Often the most we can do is "this place feels fake", or "this place has bad vibes", or "this place feels cozy". It took me many months of practice to get to the point where I could tell </span><i><span>why</span></i><span> a room made me feel off, or </span><i><span>why</span></i><span> it made me feel warm and cozy, or alert and energized. Luckily you don't need to do that because the answer most of the time turned out to be the same.</span></p><p><b><span>Usually a space feels bad because it is lit by low-quality lights</span></b></p><p><span>Our eyes evolved to see things illuminated by sunlight. Correspondingly the best proxy we have for whether the light in a room "works" is how similar the light in that room is to natural sunlight. The most popular way of measuring how much light differs from natural sunlight is the "Color Rendering Index" (CRI):</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><span>The everyday situation where I've found the effect of low-quality lighting starkest is to look at the face of a person illuminated by nothing but a computer screen. Computer screens emit extremely low CRI light, as a screen simulates white by combining the light from red, green and blue LEDs, producing a very low-CRI emission spectrum. Faces illuminated by nothing but screens often look off and have a slightly alien, plasticky, often blue-feeling quality to them, even if the screen that is emitting the light is almost fully white.</span></p><p><b><span>In short: If you want a space to feel natural, buy lightbulbs with at least 95 CRI, ideally 98. </span></b></p><hr /><p><b><span>But I thought my eyes can only see three colors?</span></b></p><p><span>Yes, and that is exactly why when you look at a computer screen directly, colors look real and vivid. Your computer screen emits light that (pretty precisely) stimulates the three kinds of cones in your eyes and so can produce basically arbitrary perceptual colors (it's not perfect, but it's quite good). </span></p><p><b><span>The problem occurs when light bounces off of other objects in the room</span></b><span>. The color of an object is determined by how it absorbs, reflects, and changes light that hits it. For example, an object under sunlight might completely absorb orange light (~630nm), but fully reflect the red light and green light emitted by the LEDs in your computer screen. That object would look unnaturally bright under the light from your computer screen, because it basically reflects all the light from your screen, but under sunlight it would absorb all orange wavelengths.</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><span>So to a first approximation a light feels "natural" if its light emission spectrum is a smooth curve. Sunlight, as well as any other light created by burning or heating things to really hot temperatures, has a smooth emission spectrum, which maintains all color information as it bounces around a room.</span></p><hr /><p><span>You might have noticed a second number that keeps showing up in the widgets above, often formatted as "5000K" or "2500K". This is the "color temperature" of light. We call this "temperature" because it corresponds to what kind of color objects emit when you heat them to that temperature</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreffszrde0h3h"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnfszrde0h3h">[2]</a></sup></span><span>. Objects that are hotter, emit light that is more blue. Objects that are less hot (e.g. "only" 2000 degrees Kelvin) emit light that is more red. Naturally we call red light "warm light" and blue light "cold light"</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefpzwgnv36uar"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnpzwgnv36uar">[3]</a></sup></span><span>.</span></p><p><b><span>If you are lighting a room with plenty of natural light, just use 2000K-3000K lights</span></b></p><p><span>People prefer bluer light during the day, but redder light during the evening and morning. Sunlight is really really bright, so what lamps you have in your room do not matter if you have large windows during the day. This means the primary purpose of your lights are to light things in the evening and morning. This means they should be warm. </span></p><p><span>If your room does not have much natural light, I recommend having bright overhead lights that are closer to 4000K, and dimmer floor lamps around 2000K-3000K.</span></p><hr /><p><b><span>The world got ugly when we invented LEDs</span></b></p><p><span>For basically all of human civilization up until very recently lighting quality was a complete non-issue. Why? Because all of our artificial light sources consisted of heating things to very hot temperatures, or burning things. When you do that, you basically always emit natural light with a smooth emission spectrum. </span></p><p><span>Lighting quality only became an issue within the last 100 years with the introduction of fluorescent lamps in offices. This is why "fluorescent lighting" has for many people become the best shorthand for fake or artificial lighting.</span></p><p><span>But people's homes, as well as any entertainment venues, bars or really anywhere where people socially congregated in the evenings were lit by incandescent light bulbs (or before then, candles and oil lamps) with perfectly smooth spectra.</span></p><p><span>But around 30 years ago home lighting LEDs were introduced, initially with truly terrible color rendering indexes, and most people unable to put words to the discomfort and alienness they caused, chose the energy-saving option and replaced their incandescent bulbs with LEDs. Eventually, in most of the western world outside of the US, incandescent lightbulbs were literally banned to promote energy saving policies. </span></p><p><span>This was the greatest uglification in history. Within two decades, much of the world that was previously filled with beautiful natural-feeling light started feeling alien, slightly off, and uncomfortable, and societal stigma around energy-saving policies prevented people from really doing anything about it.</span></p><p><span>But you, within your home, can fix this. LED technology has come along way and we can produce high-CRI LED bulbs (I recommend </span><a href="https://store.yujiintl.com/" rel="noreferrer"><span>YujiLED</span></a><span> or </span><a href="https://www.waveformlighting.com/" rel="noreferrer"><span>Waveform Lighting</span></a><span>). The world really used to be much more beautiful and a much less harsh place in this one respect. You can restore the natural light, and the homeliness that all your ancestors felt, at least within the confines of your home. Just buy some high-CRI, warm color temperature light bulbs. There is a lot more to interior design, but it's honestly so much easier to iterate on than lighting. </span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn333l30t8x95"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref333l30t8x95">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>But before that the </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/psYNRb3JCncQBjd4v/shutting-down-the-lightcone-offices" rel="noreferrer"><span>Lightcone Offices</span></a><span>, and I've also played a role in designing some of the most popular areas at </span><a href="https://constellation.org/" rel="noreferrer"><span>Constellation</span></a><span>. Also of course credit to my team for much of the work.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnfszrde0h3h"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreffszrde0h3h">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>What kind of object? Well, turns out really any object, unless the thing you are heating undergoes some specific chemical reaction when you heat them that causes them to emit other wavelengths of light. The radiation curve that most objects tend to follow here is known as the "blackbody radiation curve". You can google it or ask your local LLM if you want to understand the physics behind this better.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnpzwgnv36uar"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefpzwgnv36uar">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This is a joke. This is indeed exactly backwards. You cannot imagine how much this makes explaining color temperatures to people more confusing. "Oh, just get the warmer light bulb, no not the one that has the higher temperature written on it why would you think that, that would produce much colder light". Grrrr.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/dWib7qinqymfxevE4/if-a-room-feels-off-the-lighting-is-probably-too-spiky-or#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [The "Budgeting" Skill Has The Most Betweenness Centrality (Probably)](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/HNTm6zgCDoFDSJo6e/the-budgeting-skill-has-the-most-betweenness-centrality)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><strong>Epistemic Status:</strong> <i>Abstract claims, but grounded in data science... though the data science is somewhat stale. I wrote this on March 5th of 2026 based on memories of work I did and methods applied circa 2017, and pushed it out for publication after realizing that maybe there is appetite for it, after I saw </i><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/KRLGxCaqdgrotyB8z/there-are-only-four-skills-design-technical-management-and"><i>this post</i></a><i>.</i></p><p>Suppose we took a snapshot of each person in the US, and made a list of their "skills", as one might do with a D&amp;D character.</p><p>I would like to report on what I expect would happen if this was attempted in real life, and why (until I get to the point that you understand the title of the essay about "Budgeting" skills being Important a little ways in).</p><p>I haven't done this recently, with modern data, but I felt that this was likely to be something in my brain that most people don't know about, and worth an article.</p><p>At the end of the essay there will be a call to action! I want to start at least one study group in the SF bay area to "Level Up Budgeting" so I could attend somewhere face to face and talk about books or essays or tools, and I'd be happy if various meetup communities around the world formed their own local study groups, so there can be <a href="https://philpapers.org/rec/ZOLTEB-2">cross-pollination and transient diversity and so on</a>.</p><h2>Object Level Skill Discussion</h2><h3>First, The Boring Skills</h3><p>With a skill list for every person in the US, we would see a lot of lists for people whose chief skill is their ability to use a QWERTY keyboard and use a suite of Office Software to create spreadsheets, presentations, and prose.</p><p>It turns out you would see a lot of people who have a CPR certification, and almost everyone who gets that in modern times gets an AED certification as well.</p><p>(When I think of this, it reminds me of how rationalist!Harry went straight for a magical first aid kit on his first day of being able to buy magical gear. But also, it is interesting how "skills" blur very very swiftly into "certification of those skills" in the actual speech of actual people.)</p><p>In real life, writing down a skill list for every person would probably lead to variation in how people write.</p><p>Some people would write "Skills: word, cpr, aed, ..." and others might write "Skills &amp; Certifications: MSOffice, AED, CPR, ..." and in some platonically perfect realm these would parse down and be applied to generate <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/2jp98zdLo898qExrr/hug-the-query">the same basic inference of the same basic capacities in the author of the list</a>.</p><p>Languages already lump. To really dig into a skill, you don't just know "the name of the nameable skill" but actually can DO things.</p><p>There are probably subskills!</p><p>Some of the subskills might not even have names but still be something that can be transmitted and learned by someone saying "hold your hand &lt;like this&gt;... see?" and then "no, your pinky is getting in the way... more &lt;like this&gt;". <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/iwcgTtbARB3Pexudt/panology">We are interested in all of it, of course, from the microskills to the macro level.</a></p><h3>Second, The List Of Skills</h3><p>Most people in real life can't cast a "healing spell" (because magic isn't real, so far as I know).</p><p>And "krav maga" is pretty rare, and not what people think of first.</p><p>Fighting is not central to real life in the modern world... mostly (cops and soldiers and bouncers and bail bondsmen do make up a non-trivial chunk of the jobs though).</p><p>Usually people think of the civilian job market, in "real life", when they actually make lists of skills and post them online.</p><p>Even in the military, they have complex software helping with the task of killing people effectively to serve diplomatic ends (or whatever), but in practice they end up needing to model and optimize the logistics of hiring 18 year olds and turning <i>just the right number of them</i> into "fuel-truck drivers-license <strong>test proctors</strong>" as fast as possible. In a deep sense: a lot of skills are pretty prosaic.</p><p>The "reality" that is "out there" is <a href="https://meaningness.com/nebulosity">nebulous</a>, of course.</p><p>Depending on how much lumping and splitting you do, so far as I could tell back when I had access to the data, there are roughly 50k to 200k total skills that people will think are distinct enough to write out as distinct phrases.</p><p>Some of the phrases will point to having a specific security clearance level, but often someone might just write "Abilities: office, cpr/aed, ... <strong>security clearance</strong>, ..." as if those two words "security clearance" were a skill, or pointed to skills, or something?</p><p>(<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metonymy">Metonymy</a> is the name for what happens when people hit the limits of their words, and just name a thing for whatever thing happens to be nearby that they already know the name of.)</p><p>One could probably get the number of skills down around 35k if you put serious effect into de-duping the phrases that people use, and ignore <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hapax_legomenon">hapax legomena</a>.</p><p>But if you only de-dedupe the top 100 most common skills by hand based on semantics, and de-dupe the skill phrases that show up in skill lists algorithmically (reducing plural and single forms into one form), and keep the skill phrases that only ONE PERSON EVER thought deserved to be in a skill list, and yet also hunted far and wide to gather all the English language resumes you could (from the 1980s to 2026) I bet you could get as many as 300k skills, without much trouble.</p><p>And if you took <i>each one of these</i> and then traced it to the person, and asked them to teach someone this "officially listed skill" I bet it would turn out to often have 10 subskills that can only be described with a phrase or sentence or paragraph... which means that there are plausibly ~3M skills that are super granular and would take a "paragraph of description" to point to, potentially? Things like "the best way to hold a nail in your other hand when hammering nails".</p><p>When I estimate ~3M such skills, I'm being pretty sketch and rough... I would be surprised if it was less than 500,000 of them, and I would also be surprised if it was more than 10 million.</p><h3>Third, The Serious Lumping Begins</h3><p>A very reasonable person might think that this is crazy.</p><p>They might think "computers" is really "just one skill" and that "git" and "svn" are NOT meaningfully different skills.</p><p>They might assert that there is ONE "computer" skill, and that if you are super great at that good at the one skill (but better than a normie!) you could apply that skill in a <a href="https://www.bls.gov/oes/2023/may/oes151232.htm">tech support job</a>, but then you might level that skill up until you could apply it to <a href="https://www.bls.gov/oes/2023/may/oes151221.htm">work in Computer Research</a>.</p><p>This would be a very reasonable and pragmatic perspective, but in that case, there's a lot of prior classification work!</p><p>The reasonable people at <a href="https://www.bls.gov/oes/2023/may/oes_stru.htm">the Bureau of Labor Statistics have already lumped things down to 832 narrow job categories fitting into 116 medial grained categories and just 21 basic categories</a>.</p><p>Following them, seeking VERY lumpy lumps, you might reasonably say that the total number of "skills"...</p><p>...is either ~21 (some examples being "legal", "protection", "production", "management", "sales", and so on)...</p><p>...or else perhaps ~116 (some examples being "legal support skills", "fire fighting skills", "printing skills", "operational management skills", or "wholesale sales skills").</p><p>The BLS... is almost certainly too narrow.</p><p>Those aren't <i>really</i> skills, you know?</p><p>Those are <strong>really</strong> more like <i>job categories</i>... right?</p><p>But it does seem to be true that job categories sort of wink and nudge at the sense in which some skills might be more convergently useful than other skills, or have prerequisite skills, and be useful mostly only in concert with other skills.</p><p>Most people have played the piano at least a little, when killing time and kinda bored while waiting somewhere that a piano existed, and quite a few people have practiced specific piano skills (and there are many distinct such skills to practice separately (like just sitting properly, even)) but if we bow down to the lumpiest lumpers and their traditions, even using the full 832 BLS categories, <i>all(!) of the piano skills</i> (and so much more) are lumped into "<a href="https://www.bls.gov/oes/2022/may/oes272042.htm">27-2042  Musicians and Singers</a>".</p><p>This is NOT GOOD ENOUGH to tell us what skills to spend 10 minutes practicing every day, or how to hire people who will pay the piano in the way that we really want to hear it played in a bar from just what can be found about them via online scraping of their data.</p><p>If we are going to HIRE for jobs, based on <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/B7P97C27rvHPz3s9B/gears-in-understanding">a gears level understanding</a> of what cognitive or physical performance or capacity goes on in that job then we need something <i>more granular</i> than jobs to point at, or reason about, that "are the gears".</p><h3>Fourth, Lumping The Skills With Math?!?</h3><p>Here's a thought: maybe we take <i><strong>all the skill lists as is</strong></i> to create ~100k skills from some corpus (with regional choices, and choice of era, and some amount of de-duping) that someone ever mentioned.</p><p>We treat each unique skill skill as a node in a network (or as mathematicians might call it a "graph theoretic graph")... and then for each skill list in each resume, we draw lines between every pair of skills that occur in the the same list.</p><p>And then for the next list we do the same (making some lines +1 in strength if they were paired in a previous list, so AED and CPR are decently likely to have a three-weight-strong link after processing maybe a dozen random resumes).</p><p>This gives us a weighted graph, which comes up in a LOT of optimization problems like <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shortest_path_problem">shortest path discovery</a> and <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PageRank">pagerank</a> and so on.</p><p>I have done this before! That's why <i>I</i> am writing this essay ;-)</p><p>Lots of "clusters" of skills fall out of such an analysis in the form of dense <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clique_(graph_theory)">cliques</a> where many members of the clique are strongly linked to other members of the clique, and weakly linked to anything else. These cliques represent tasks that are demanded in the same job, or abilities taught in very common forms of general education, or sometimes tasks that show up in "standard career progresses" (where a drafting student becomes an engineering tech and eventually a licensed surveyor, and their skill list has things from that whole history).</p><p>Every so often you'll find skills that are very common, and that many cliques of skills ALL point to.</p><p>For example, there's a bunch of skills that teachers, in the field of education, are proud of, where, by mid career, almost everyone is bragging on their resume that they can do "curriculum design" (no matter whether they're a math teacher or a piano teacher or a kindergarten teacher).</p><p>There are other skills like "security clearance" that might show up in the protection area..</p><p>But then "cpr" is linked to both of these (and many others)!</p><p>This strongly suggests a high degree of convergent instrumental utility exists in skills like this, across a wide variety of fields, even though the skill is "narrowly a skill" that is more like "<a href="https://graphite.com/blog/every-engineer-should-understand-git-reflog">git reflogging</a>" that can be taught and practiced and tested, but also "broadly a skill" in the sense that it comes up for practically everyone.</p><h3>Fifth, Seeking "Betweenness" Centrality You'll Find "Budgeting"</h3><p>There are many measures of "centrality" in graph theory.</p><p>One way to arrange the math of it might pinch out the thing at the <i>very center</i> of the <strong>very biggest clique</strong> (which itself is perhaps at the center of the biggest macro-clique and so on)... but that won't give us these insanely broadly applicable skills that can be taught and learned!</p><p>The thing we want, if we're looking for very very broadly valuable skills in almost any domain or any job is <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Betweenness_centrality"><i>betweenness centrality</i></a>.</p><p>The way this works (roughly) is that we pick a lot of pairs of nodes, and spend compute to find the shortest path between them... over and over... and every time we do this we add a point to all the nodes that were on this "shortest journey" from node to node.</p><p>At the end, we find the chokepoint... the master node, the node from which you can go ALMOST ANYWHERE very very quickly.</p><p>"Curriculum design" is more central than "math curriculum" because it is invoked by more kinds of people with more diversity of skills. And "CPR" has still more betweenness centrality than "curriculum design" (because CPR is useful for cops with cop skills, and life guards with swim instructor skills, and fire fighters, but also elementary school teachers, and summer camp instructors and so on).</p><p>Here is the punchline: the thing with the most betweenneess centrality out of all skills is "budgeting".</p><p>Which... uh... you know... makes sense? Maybe? &lt;3</p><h3>Sixth, Meditations On The Betweenness Centrality Of "Budgeting"</h3><p>Here are some thoughts:</p><p>FIRST, consider the holy grail of "rationality" (in the sense of "verbally transmissible cognitive practices that conduce to higher chances of success at nearly any goal" is <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transferable_skill">skill transfer</a>).</p><p>Finding skills with high skill transfer and broad applicability lets you spend the least amount of time leveling up, and gives you the most benefit from them.</p><p>The skill of "budgeting" makes sense here because like... finitude is everywhere? Tradeoffs are everywhere? Also time is real and ubiquitous. And "making tradeoffs over time in the face of scarcity" is basically the essence of budgeting.</p><p>"Budgeting" arguably <i>deserves</i> some halo, because it makes sense, from first principles, that almost every agent would need this skill, if you actually think about agency itself in a first principles way.</p><p>SECOND, this comes up <i>in practice in business</i> because <a href="https://spinuzzi.blogspot.com/2007/01/reading-mangle-of-practice.html">the "mangle" of organizations</a> often leads to a handful of people on like..  "the budgeting committee" (which no one wants to be on because it SOUNDS SO BORING) wielding enormous organizational power, and needing to be able to justify their use of power when budgets cause weekly, monthly, quarterly, yearly adjustments in what the organization can and will choose to do.</p><p>This will show up, predictably, at the HEART of many <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moral_Mazes">moral mazes</a>. We would kind of expect, then, like with the "security clearance" skill, that "budgeting" is a pointer to skills related to the use of language in clarifying <i>AND</i> politically obfuscating ways, depending on who a budgeter is talking to.</p><p>The skill might be <a href="https://slatestarcodex.com/2015/01/11/the-phatic-and-the-anti-inductive/">anti-inductively hard to master</a> <i>because</i> it is woven into bizlore (not academia) and it is very political by default.</p><p>THIRD, the lists of skills from which this kind of conclusion can be drawn often come from a wide variety of people, posting resumes online and such. Some of them are older, and more advanced in their careers.</p><p>There is a decent chance that if you start in almost any profession or organization, and are good at anything, eventually you'll rise to a role of teaching, guidance, planning, and <i>in a word</i> "leadership". And almost all leaders of non-trivial organizations have accounting and bookkeeping to handle... and also this idea that "the procedures of accounting" could be used to map and understand and control and generally guide a team?</p><p>So maybe the betweenness centrality of budgeting comes from a real pinch point, that shows up over and over again in MANY careers, where "budgeting" (about your expertise, on behalf of a group that collectively wields that expertise) becomes essential.</p><p>FOURTH, we can compare Biden's semi-famous quote "<a href="https://x.com/POTUS46Archive/status/1508530790249947147">Don’t tell me what you value. Show me your budget—and I’ll tell you what you value</a>" with a lot of common topics on LW like <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ZpDnRCeef2CLEFeKM/money-the-unit-of-caring">money itself (the unit of caring!)</a> and <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/kelly-criterion">kelly betting (essentially iterated bet budgeting)</a>, and <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/YZeZXF6LwZn5vqFo9/the-fundamental-theorem-of-asset-pricing-missing-link-of-the">the relation of money to VNM rational utility functions and bayesian beliefs</a>.</p><p>If the skill of "budgeting" turned out to be a super power wouldn't <i>actually</i> be <i>that</i> surprising, if you have <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Nu3wa6npK4Ry66vFp/a-sense-that-more-is-possible">a sense that more is possible</a>.</p><h2>A Sort Of A Methods Section</h2><p>This essay so far has been very high level. I haven't really substantiated it, except to casually suggest what MIGHT be observed, if someone hypothetically looked at certain ways of describing the world in an intensely operational way. Maybe you just don't think it makes sense?</p><p>I generally don't like talking about myself, but... uh...</p><p>I applied to work at Google in 2013 when I believed that they had the Mandate Of Heaven with respect to the Singularity.</p><p>(For several reasons. One big one was that I had previously thought The Singularity Institute had the Mandate Of Heaven earlier than that, but then they sold their banner and trademark and social outreach apparatus to Kurzweil in 2012, who worked at Google. This was before OpenAI was founded in 2014. If I had waited another year, I might have joined OpenAI, instead.)</p><p>By 2018 I had given up on Google, because it is a moral maze, and was clearly not going to save the world, and I moved on to working on a blockchain project with friends that was <i>not</i> much related to the Singularity or to AGI in any obvious ways, but it gave me an opportunity to design decentralized utopias and dystopias for decent pay, and that was just super super hard to turn down.</p><p>In the intervening time, from 2013 to 2017, I first worked inside the Google Brain "cultural silo" (because I had been told I could work on deep learning aimed at comprehensive proactive benevolence) but after about a year of working on bullshit (like trying to get people to watch longer YouTube content that ads could fit in the middle of) I changed to work on something that actually fucking matters.</p><p>So I hopped over into the "PeopleOps" silo by 2015, and instead of increasing the size of YouTube's "ad inventory" I helped build <a href="https://www.google.com/search?udm=8&amp;q=ai jobs in Seattle">the job search engine</a> that is one of many topic-focused engines existing inside the overall Google Machine for that overall technological macro object to wield according to high level metrics.</p><p>(Job search seemed like the closest thing I would be able to find to <a href="https://tvtropes.org/pmwiki/pmwiki.php/Literature/TheSwordOfGood">a sword of good</a>, that The Google Machine would have a hard time abusing <i>too</i> egregiously. And it seemed like it could plausibly produce tens of thousands of dollars in consumer surplus for people who used "free job search" (supported merely by ad money, that is) to find better jobs.)</p><p>For about 18 months, between 2015-2017, I was doing data science on the theory and practice of searching for jobs <i>specifically</i> through the lens of "skills and education".</p><p>I didn't have the ability to contact people in the outside world about it (because we didn't want to leak that we were working on job search and so on) but my team had access to "all the resumes" and "all the job postings"... in English anyway (and it turns out that the language used in different job markets around the US was regionally dialectical, and so we optimized at the beginning for just a handful of cities, and when we tried to use machine translation to generalize it to Telugu or Japanese the relevance numbers just totally cratered because there is a lot of nuance in the euphemisms people use to talk about hiring and firing and such).</p><p>We could play with ways to parse and analyze them using now-old-fashioned pre-LLM NLP techniques. So for a bit more than a year, I could, in fact, do the kind of analyses I described above, but with real data.</p><p>And "budgeting" did, in fact, fall out of that actual data, back in ~2017, in the way described &lt;3</p><h2>A Call To Action: Study Groups</h2><p>Does anyone else want to do this?</p><p>I hope that people in the comments chime in in lots of ways because I still feel like "budgeting" is something I'm STILL learning to do well. I would love to hear good textbooks. I would love to hear war stories. I would love to hear ways to spend an hour a day practicing something for a week and become really good at "budgeting".</p><p>Does anyone know how to hire the best CFO who is a total wizard at the "budgeting" skill? I don't! I think that's an important thing to be able to do, and I can't reliably do it.</p><p>I would be open to driving to Berkeley once a week to talk about some shared reading, or report on homework we assigned ourselves, and hear about other people's challenges and growth at "budgeting, the most central of skills" &lt;3</p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/HNTm6zgCDoFDSJo6e/the-budgeting-skill-has-the-most-betweenness-centrality#comments">Discuss</a>

## Engineering

### [Fragments: April 14](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-04-14.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>I attended the first Pragmatic Summit early this year, and while there host
<a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CZs8J1ZD0CE">Gergely Orosz interviewed Kent Beck and myself on stage</a>. The video runs for about half-an-hour.</p>

<p><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CZs8J1ZD0CE"><img alt="A photo of Gergely, Kent, and myself on the stage. We are all bedazzled by the vibrancy of Kent's trousers" src="https://martinfowler.com/img/catalog/2026-prag-summit.png" /></a></p>

<p>I always enjoy nattering with Kent like this, and Gergely pushed into some worthwhile topics. Given
the timing, AI dominated the conversation - we compared it to earlier
technology shifts, the experience of agile methods, the role of TDD, the
danger of unhealthy performance metrics, and how to thrive in an AI-native
industry.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Perl is a language I used a little, but never loved. However the definitive book on it, by its designer Larry Wall, contains a wonderful gem. The three virtues of a programmer: hubris, impatience - and above all - <em>laziness</em>.</p>

<p>Bryan Cantrill <a href="https://bcantrill.dtrace.org/2026/04/12/the-peril-of-laziness-lost/">also loves this virtue</a>:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Of these virtues, I have always found laziness to be the most profound: packed within its tongue-in-cheek self-deprecation is a commentary on not just the need for abstraction, but the aesthetics of it. Laziness drives us to make the system as simple as possible (but no simpler!) — to develop the powerful abstractions that then allow us to do much more, much more easily.</p>

  <p>Of course, the implicit wink here is that it takes a lot of work to be lazy</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Understanding how to think about a problem domain by building abstractions (models) is my favorite part of programming. I love it because I think it’s what gives me a deeper understanding of a problem domain, and because once I find a good set of abstractions, I get a buzz from the way they make difficulties melt away, allowing me to achieve much more functionality with less lines of code.</p>

<p>Cantrill worries that AI is so good at writing code, we risk losing that virtue, something that’s reinforced by brogrammers bragging about how they produce thirty-seven thousand lines of code a day.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The problem is that LLMs inherently lack the virtue of laziness. Work costs nothing to an LLM. LLMs do not feel a need to optimize for their own (or anyone’s) future time, and will happily dump more and more onto a layercake of garbage. Left unchecked, LLMs will make systems larger, not better — appealing to perverse vanity metrics, perhaps, but at the cost of everything that matters. As such, LLMs highlight how essential our human laziness is: our finite time forces us to develop crisp abstractions in part because we don’t want to waste our (human!) time on the consequences of clunky ones. The best engineering is always borne of constraints, and the constraint of our time places limits on the cognitive load of the system that we’re willing to accept. This is what drives us to make the system simpler, despite its essential complexity.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>This reflection particularly struck me this Sunday evening. I’d spent a bit of time making a modification of how my music playlist generator worked. I needed a new capability, spent some time adding it, got frustrated at how long it was taking, and wondered about maybe throwing a coding agent at it. More thought led to realizing that I was doing it in a more complicated way than it needed to be. I was including a facility that I didn’t need, and <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/Yagni.html">by applying yagni</a>, I could make the whole thing much easier, doing the task in just a couple of dozen lines of code.</p>

<p>If I had used an LLM for this, it may well have done the task much more quickly, but would it have made a similar over-complication? If so would I just shrug and say LGTM? Would that complication cause me (or the LLM) problems in the future?</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Jessica Kerr (Jessitron) has a simple example of <a href="https://jessitron.com/2026/04/06/adding-correctness-conditions-to-code-changes/">applying the principle of Test-Driven Development to prompting agents</a>. She wants all updates to include updating the documentation.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Instructions – We can change AGENTS.md to instruct our coding agent to look for documentation files and update them.</p>

  <p>Verification – We can add a reviewer agent to check each PR for missed documentation updates.</p>

  <p>This is two changes, so I can break this work into two parts. Which of these should we do first?</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Of course my initial comment about TDD answers that question</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Mark Little prodded an old memory of mine as he wondered about to work with AIs that are over-confident of their knowledge and thus prone to make up answers to questions, or to act when they should be more hesitant. He draws inspiration from an old, low-budget, but classic SciFi movie: <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dark_Star_(film)">Dark Star</a>. I saw that movie once in my 20s (ie a long time ago), but I still remember the crisis scene where a crew member has to use <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=S-xUjmJkO8g">philosophical argument to prevent a sentient bomb from detonating</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Doolittle: You have no absolute proof that Sergeant Pinback ordered you to detonate.<br />
Bomb #20:  I recall distinctly the detonation order. My memory is good on matters like these.<br />
Doolittle: Of course you remember it, but all you remember is merely a series of sensory impulses which you now realize have no real, definite connection with outside reality.<br />
Bomb #20:  True. But since this is so, I have no real proof that you’re telling me all this.<br />
Doolittle: That’s all beside the point. I mean, the concept is valid no matter where it originates.<br />
Bomb #20:  Hmmmm….<br />
Doolittle: So, if you detonate…<br />
Bomb #20:  In nine seconds….<br />
Doolittle: …you could be doing so on the basis of false data.<br />
Bomb #20:  I have no proof it was false data.<br />
Doolittle: You have no proof it was correct data!<br />
Bomb #20:  I must think on this further.<br /></p>
</blockquote>

<p>Doolittle has to expand the bomb’s consciousness, teaching it to doubt its sensors. <a href="https://markclittle.blogspot.com/2026/03/dark-star-and-ai-morality.html">As Little puts it:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>That’s a useful metaphor for where we are with AI today. Most AI systems are optimised for decisiveness. Given an input, produce an output. Given ambiguity, resolve it probabilistically. Given uncertainty, infer. This works well in bounded domains, but it breaks down in open systems where the cost of a wrong decision is asymmetric or irreversible. In those cases, the correct behaviour is often deferral, or even deliberate inaction. But inaction is not a natural outcome of most AI architectures. It has to be designed in.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>In my more human interactions, I’ve always valued doubt, and distrust people who operate under undue certainty. Doubt doesn’t necessarily lead to indecisiveness, but it does suggest that we include the risk of inaccurate information or faulty reasoning into decisions with profound consequences.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>If we want AI systems that can operate safely without constant human oversight, we need to teach them not just how to decide, but when not to. In a world of increasing autonomy, restraint isn’t a limitation, it’s a capability. And in many cases, it may be the most important one we build.</p>
</blockquote>

### [Fragments: April  9](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-04-09.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>I mostly link to written material here, but I’ve recently listened to two excellent podcasts that I can recommend.</p>

<p>Anyone who regularly reads these fragments knows that I’m a big fan of Simon Willison, his (also very fragmentary) posts have earned a regular spot in my RSS reader. But the problem with fragments, however valuable, is that they don’t provide a cohesive overview of the situation. So <a href="https://www.lennysnewsletter.com/p/an-ai-state-of-the-union">his podcast with Lenny Rachitsky</a> is a welcome survey of that state of world as seen through a discerning pair of eyeballs. He paints a good picture of how programming has changed for him since the “November inflection point”, important patterns for this work, and his concern about the security bomb nestled inside the beast.</p>

<p>My other great listening was on a regular podcast that I listen to, as <a href="https://newsletter.pragmaticengineer.com/p/scaling-uber-with-thuan-pham-ubers">Gergely Orosz interviewed Thuan Pham</a> - the former CTO of Uber. As with so many of Gergely’s podcasts, they focused on Thuan Pham’s fascinating career direction, giving listeners an opportunity to learn from a successful professional. There’s also an informative insight into Uber’s use of microservices (they had 5000 of them), and the way high-growth software necessarily gets rewritten a lot (a phenomenon I dubbed <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/SacrificialArchitecture.html">Sacrificial Architecture</a>)</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Axios published their <a href="https://simonwillison.net/2026/Apr/3/supply-chain-social-engineering/">post-mortem on their recent supply chain compromise</a>. It’s quite a story, the attackers spent a couple of weeks developing contact with the lead maintainer, leading to a video call where the meeting software indicated something on the maintainer’s system was out of date. That led to the maintainer installing the update, which in fact was a Remote Access Trojan (RAT).</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>they tailored this process specifically to me by doing the following:</p>

  <ul>
    <li>they reached out masquerading as the founder of a company they had cloned the companys founders likeness as well as the company itself.</li>
    <li>they then invited me to a real slack workspace. this workspace was branded to the companies ci and named in a plausible manner. the slack was thought out very well, they had channels where they were sharing linked-in posts, the linked in posts i presume just went to the real companys account but it was super convincing etc. they even had what i presume were fake profiles of the team of the company but also number of other oss maintainers.</li>
    <li>they scheduled a meeting with me to connect. the meeting was on ms teams. the meeting had what seemed to be a group of people that were involved.</li>
    <li>the meeting said something on my system was out of date. i installed the missing item as i presumed it was something to do with teams, and this was the RAT.</li>
    <li>everything was extremely well co-ordinated looked legit and was done in a professional manner.</li>
  </ul>
</blockquote>

<p>Simon Willison has a <a href="https://simonwillison.net/2026/Apr/3/supply-chain-social-engineering/">summary and further links</a>.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I recently bumped into <a href="https://diataxis.fr/how-to-use-diataxis/">Diátaxis</a>, a framework for organizing technical documentation. I only looked at it briefly, but there’s much to like. In particular I appreciated how it classified four forms of documentation:</p>

<ul>
  <li>Tutorials: to learn how to use the product</li>
  <li>How-to guides: for users to follow to achieve particular goals with the product</li>
  <li>Reference: to describe what the product does</li>
  <li>Explanations: background and context to educate the user on the product’s rationale</li>
</ul>

<p>The distinction between tutorials and how-to guides is interesting</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>A tutorial serves the needs of the user who is at study. Its obligation is to provide a successful learning experience. A how-to guide serves the needs of the user who is at work. Its obligation is to help the user accomplish a task.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>I also appreciated its point of pulling explanations out into separate areas. The idea is that other forms should contain only minimal explanations, linking to the explanation material for more depth. That way we keep the flow on the goal and allow the user to seek deeper explanations in their own way. The study/work distinction between explanation and reference mirrors that same distinction between tutorials and how-to guides.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>For eight years, Lalit Maganti wanted a set of tools for working with SQLite. But it would be hard and tedious work, “getting into the weeds of SQLite source code, a fiendishly difficult codebase to understand”. So he didn’t try it. But after the <a href="https://simonwillison.net/2026/Jan/4/inflection/">November inflection point</a>, he decided to tackle this need.</p>

<p><a href="https://lalitm.com/post/building-syntaqlite-ai/">His account of this exercise</a> is an excellent description of the benefits and perils of developing with AI agents.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Through most of January, I iterated, acting as semi-technical manager and delegating almost all the design and all the implementation to Claude. Functionally, I ended up in a reasonable place: a parser in C extracted from SQLite sources using a bunch of Python scripts, a formatter built on top, support for both the SQLite language and the PerfettoSQL extensions, all exposed in a web playground.</p>

  <p>But when I reviewed the codebase in detail in late January, the downside was obvious: the codebase was complete spaghetti. I didn’t understand large parts of the Python source extraction pipeline, functions were scattered in random files without a clear shape, and a few files had grown to several thousand lines. It was extremely fragile; it solved the immediate problem but it was never going to cope with my larger vision, never mind integrating it into the Perfetto tools. The saving grace was that it had proved the approach was viable and generated more than 500 tests, many of which I felt I could reuse.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He threw it all away and worked more closely with the AI on the second attempt, with lots of thinking about the design, reviewing all the code, and refactoring with every step</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>In the rewrite, refactoring became the core of my workflow. After every large batch of generated code, I’d step back and ask “is this ugly?” Sometimes AI could clean it up. Other times there was a large-scale abstraction that AI couldn’t see but I could; I’d give it the direction and let it execute. If you have taste, the cost of a wrong approach drops dramatically because you can restructure quickly.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He ended up with a working system, and the AI proved its value in allowing him to tackle something that he’d been leaving on the todo pile for years. But even with the rewrite, the AI had its potholes.</p>

<p>His conclusion of the relative value of AI in different scenarios:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>When I was working on something I already understood deeply, AI was excellent….
When I was working on something I could describe but didn’t yet know, AI was good but required more care….
When I was working on something where I didn’t even know what I wanted, AI was somewhere between unhelpful and harmful…</p>
</blockquote>

<p>At the heart of this is that AI works at its best when there is an objectively checkable answer. If we want an implementation that can pass some tests, then AI does a good job. But when it came to the public API:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>I spent several days in early March doing nothing but API refactoring, manually fixing things any experienced engineer would have instinctively avoided but AI made a total mess of. There’s no test or objective metric for “is this API pleasant to use” and “will this API help users solve the problems they have” and that’s exactly why the coding agents did so badly at it.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I became familiar with Ryan Avent’s writing when he wrote the Free Exchange column for The Economist. <a href="https://ryanavent.substack.com/p/faith-and-the-future">His recent post</a> talks about how James Talarico and Zohran Mamdani have made their religion an important part of their electoral appeal, and their faith is centered on caring for others. He explains that a focus on care leads to an important perspective on economic growth.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The first thing to understand is that we should not want growth for its own sake. What is good about growth is that it expands our collective capacities: we come to know more and we are able to do more. This, in turn, allows us to alleviate suffering, to discover more things about the universe, and to spend more time being complete people.</p>
</blockquote>
